iii 
 Preface 
This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India for the 
period ended March 2023 has been prepared for submission to the 
Governor of the State of Himachal Pradesh under Article 151 of the 
Constitution of India. 
The Report contains significant results of Information Technology 
Audit, Performance and Compliance Audit of  Receipts and 
Expenditure of the Government of Himachal Pradesh and review of 
Financial Performance of  State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs) , 
conducted in terms of the Comptroller and Auditor General of 
India's (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Services) Act, 1971.  
Period covered in the Information Technology Audit is 2018-23, in 
Performance Audit is 201 8-22 and in Subject Specific Compliance 
Audit is 2018-21 and in review of Financial Performance of SPSEs 
is 2022-23. 
The audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing 
Standards issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Overview 
 
 
  

v 
 
 
 
This Report covers matters arising out of the Information Technology Audit on 
e-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh, Performance Audit (PA) on E-Way Bill System, 
Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) on Departme nt’s Oversight on GST 
Payments and Returns Filing for the Years 2018-19 to 2020-21 and review of Financial 
Performance of State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs). The Report has been organised 
into five chapters as under:  
Chapter I - General 
This is an introductory chapter, which contains trends of revenue receipts, analysis of 
arrears of revenue, arrears in assessments, evasion of tax, refund cases, response of 
Government towards Audit, planning and conduct of A udit and follow -up on Audit 
Reports. 
Information Technology Audit 
 
Chapter II – E-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh 
The Information Technology Audit of the e-Procurement system in Himachal Pradesh 
was conducted to evaluate the effectiveness, transparency, and accountability of the 
digital tendering processes implemented across Government Departments. Initiated in 
2011 as a pilot under the National e -Governance Plan (NeGP), the e -Procurement 
system aimed to streamline procurement through internet -based technologies and 
ensure fair competition. Although a State Government notific ation (June 2018) 
mandated all Departments and Government Bodies to use the e-Procurement portal for 
tenders above ₹ five lakh, the audit revealed critical gaps in implementation and system 
usage. 
The audit covered the period from April 2018 to March 2023 and analysed the data in 
respect of 77,152 tenders across 83 procuring entities. Using a combination of data 
analytics, application control reviews, and scrutiny of manual and online records, the 
Audit identified significant deficiencies. Stratified random sampling was employed t o 
select three districts, nine D epartments, 51 units, and 783 tenders for detailed 
examination. 
Key findings: 
Incomplete implementation: Audit had highlighted the non-implementation of various 
modules of the e-Procurement system in Audit Report – Social, General and Economic 
Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2017. At that time, only one module – 
e-Tendering was functioning. However, e ven after seven years,  the e-Procurement 
system was limited to partial implementation of three more modules ( vendor 
management, e -Auction and e -Payment), while three modules, such as indent 
management, catalogue management, and contract management were not implemented 
(September 2024).  
Overview 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
vi 
The absence of a formal Service Level Agreement (SLA) with National Informatics 
Centre (NIC) and irregular meetings of the Core Committee impeded monitoring and 
effective roll-out.  
Sub-optimal utilisation: The system’s full potential remains untapped, as 16 out of 100 
procuring entities, such as Dr. Rajendra Prasad Government Medical College Tanda, 
Directorate of Youth Services and Sports, Himachal Pradesh Vidhan Sabha, Himachal 
Pradesh Institute of Public Administration, etc. did not use the e -Procurement portal 
since inception and 15 others published fewer than ten contracts as of March 2023. 
Further, several key entries were manually entered by department users rather than 
being automatically captured by the e-Procurement system, leading to inconsistencies 
between the system’s data and the actual transactions. 
Systemic deficiencies: The platform lacked critical functionalities required under the 
Himachal Pradesh Financial Rules (HPFR) and department-specific timelines, such as 
mapping of prescribed timelines for bids submission, enforcement of the minimum 
number of bids, and the captu ring of rate contract details. This led to poor lifecycle 
tracking of tenders, procedural delays, and non -compliance with bid processing 
timelines. Data integrity and control issues: Inadequate input controls allowed 
erroneous entrie s, such as incomplete bidder details, decryption failures, and 
inconsistent tender stage updates, compromising audit trails and data reliability.  
Further, legal sanctity of Digital Signature Certificate (DSCs) and legitimacy of bids 
was not ensured due to improper mapping of the DSC with the user ID and name.  
Lack of automated red flags: Crucially, the system did not incorporate real -time alerts 
for anomalies, procedural violations, or suspicious patterns. This allowed serious lapses 
to go undetected. 
The objectives of training and support through the Project Management Unit were only 
partially met. The training was limited to the initial phase in December 2011, where 81 
bidders and 220 department officials were trained. No records were available for 
training activities during 2018–2023. 
Indicators of collusion and bid rigging: Audit analytics flagged potential indicators of 
anti-competitive practices, including: 
• Multiple bids from identical or Departmental IP addresses within short intervals. 
• Marginal bid variation suggesting rotational cartel behaviour. 
• Award of tenders at inflated rates and to non-L1 bidders. 
• Instances of tender splitting to avoid threshold-based scrutiny. 
These red flags were neither detected nor acted upon by the system, underscoring the 
need for integrated fraud detection tools. 
Conclusion 
The e -Procurement system in Himachal Pradesh has not yet achieved its intended 
objectives of transparency, efficiency, and fairness in public procurement. Technical 
shortcomings, inadequate governance mechanisms, and absence of automated 

Overview 
vii 
compliance checks continue to compromise the integrity of the procurement process. 
Urgent reforms including formalisation of operational frameworks, integration of 
rule-based validations, and adoption of real -time analytics and alert systems are 
essential for ensuring robust and accountable e-Procurement in the State. 
The Government may: 
1. Revitalise the Core Committee with regular meetings, defined roles, and clear 
follow-up protocols to oversee system implementation, policy decisions, and issue 
resolution. 
2. Enhance the system to fully su pport compliance with HPFR and 
Department-specific procurement timelines, including auto -tracking and alerts 
for delays. 
3. Strengthen input controls to prevent incorrect or incomplete data entries and 
ensure data consistency and integrity across modules. 
4. Incorporate real-time updates of tender stages and automated summary reports 
to reduce manual intervention and improve transparency through enhanced 
automation.  
5. Conduct regular training sessions for both procuring entity officials and bidders, 
with a focus on system usage, bid submission, and security protocols. Establish 
a responsive helpdesk to provide technical and procedural support to users. 
6. Implement robust input and validation controls to ensure completeness of user 
data while registration and proper mapping of Digital Signature Certificate s 
(DSCs) with user IDs, thereby upholding the legal sanctity of DSCs and the 
legitimacy of bids. 
7. Develop algorithms to flag potential collusion and suspicious bidding patterns 
such as use of identical IPs, bid timing overlaps, price collusion, repetitive award 
patterns, or unusual bidding behaviour. 
8. Reduce the threshold limit for e -Tendering across all D epartments to promote 
wider adoption of e-Procurement and prevent tender splitting. 
Performance Audit 
 
Chapter III - E-Way Bill System 
The Himachal Pradesh State Taxes & Excise D epartment has 16 units in the S tate to 
examine the taxpayer’s compliance, scrutiny process and subsequent preventive action 
taken on E-Way Bill (EWB) raised by the c onsignor/consignee/transporter. A sample 
of 127 EWBs of 52 taxpayers in nine units for assessing the effectiveness of EWB 
mechanism in protecting revenue interest of the Government and 200 cases for 
enforcement/preventive activities of the Department for the period from April 2018 to 
March 2022 were selected for audit. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
viii 
During the Performance Audit of EWB System under GST for the State of Himachal 
Pradesh, Audit observed certain deficiencies in the EWB system. 
It was observed that the EWB system could not distinguish between inter -state and 
intra-state movement of goods by composition taxpayers and permitted generation of 
EWBs in both cases. There were instances where EWBs were generated but taxpayers 
filed nil GSTR -1 for the respective return periods and did not discharge their tax 
liability. It was further noticed that EWBs were generated by non-filer taxpayers whose 
registrations were cancelled without settling the tax liability.  
Audit observed that EWB portal permitted generation of multiple EWB on same 
invoice. In many cases, it was observed that although the taxpayer had filed GST returns 
regularly but there was short-payment or non-payment of tax liabilities, resulting in loss 
of ₹4.43 crore to the Government exchequer.  
A test-check of 120 EWBs of 45 taxpayers indicated that nine taxpayers across four 
units had availed Input Tax Credit (ITC) of ₹335.26 crore against the inward supplies, 
whereas the ITC actually available to them as per GSTR -2A was only ₹314.92 crore. 
This resulted in excess availment of ITC amounting to ₹20.34 crore and consequent 
loss of revenue to the Government exchequer. 
During the evaluation of the enforcement/ preventive activities viz. , operational 
preparedness, effectiveness of anti -evasion measures and intra -department and 
inter-department coordination, it was observed that the Department had not established 
a dedicated formation for EWB verification which may have improved the efficiency 
of preventive functions.  
It was further observed that the absence of patrol vehicl es for EWB verification in the 
Department may have impacted operational efficiency and resulted in limited mobility 
and making on-site verifications challenging. In case of some units, the Department did 
not have specific targets for conducting verification of EWBs. 
Preventive units were not using the analytical reports designed by NIC for planning the 
vehicle’s interception. These Reports were not shared with the jurisdictional officers 
for using them in scrutiny of returns.  
The Government may: 
1. Incorporate validation control in EWB system to alert the taxpayer as well as the 
Departmental officer when a taxpayer under Composition Levy Scheme 
generates EWB for inter-state supply. 
2. Issue suitable instructions to the Proper O fficers for considering EWBs 
generated by the taxpayer before cancellation of registration retrospectively and 
taking action for recovery of tax, wherever applicable. 
3. Incorporate validation control in the EW B system to alert taxpayer and 
Departmental officer on generation of multiple EWBs with same invoice/similar 
invoice. 

Overview 
ix 
4. Ensure that t he analytical reports generated by National Informatics Centre 
(NIC) on risky EWB transactions may be utilised by Departmental officers for 
identifying probable cases of tax evasion.  
Subject Specific Compliance Audit 
 
Chapter IV - Department’s Oversight on GST Payments a nd Returns Filing for 
the Year 2018-19 to 2020-21 
The Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) on Departmental Oversight on Goods 
and Service Tax (GST) Payments and Return Filing was undertaken , in the context of 
varying trends of return filing and continued data inconsistencies, with an objective of 
assessing the adequacy of the system in monitoring return filing and tax payments, 
extent of compliance and other Departmental oversight functions.  
This SSCA was predominantly based on data analysis, which highlighted risk areas, 
red flags and in some cases, rule -based deviations and logical inconsistencies in GST 
returns filed for the period between April 2018 and March 2021. The SSCA entailed 
assessing the oversight functions of State jurisdictional formation at two levels – at the 
data level through global data queries and at the functional level with a deeper detailed 
audit of the Circles and of the GST returns. The audit sample selected 348 taxpay ers 
for limited audit and 50 taxpayers for detailed audit of GST returns for the period 
2018-21. 
The Department issued Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) in May 2023 for scrutiny 
of returns. The Department was only pursuing GST returns related inconsistencies 
identified by Economic Intelligence Unit (EIU). A review of the 10 Circles for the 
period from April 2020 to March 2021 disclosed that documentation of e ssential 
oversight functions of Circles such as monitoring of return filing, taxpayer compliance 
was poor and not amenable to evaluation. As such, the functions of the Circles were 
neither fully digitised nor carried out in an organised manner. 
Audit noticed significant delays and deficiencies in the recovery of dues assessed under 
GST. In 256 cases across ten Circles, demand orders in Form DRC -07 for recovery of 
₹49.56 crore were issued during 2020-21. However, despite the lapse of more than three 
years, the required follow -up action for recovery was not effectively pursued by the 
department. This  is evident from the fact that only  a sum of ₹2.09 crore had been 
recovered in 75 cases. 
Analysis of MIS data on taxpayers who accepted TDS credit but filed nil GSTR -3B 
returns, showed that during 2020-21, 323 taxpayers across 10 Circles accepted TDS of 
₹2.43 crore through 569 returns, though they reported nil taxable turnover in GSTR-3B. 
This indicates a probable suppression of ₹121.33 crore of taxable turnover, involving 
an estimated GST liability of ₹21.84 crore (at 18 per cent). 
The Department responded to 420 out of 627 deviations of high value data 
inconsistencies identified by Audit. Of these, 272 cases constituting 64.76  per cent, 
turned out to be compliance deficiencies with mismatches (including turnover) 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
x 
amounting to ₹590.86  crore. A relatively higher rate of def iciencies was noticed in 
short/non-payment of interest, Input Tax Credit (ITC) mismatch, excess Reverse 
Charge Mechanism (RCM) ITC availed and incorrect turnover declarations. Out of the 
420 cases, the Department has recovered ₹3.72 crore in 16 cases and raised a demand 
of ₹69.57 crore in 50 cases. Additionally, notices amounting to ₹318.79 crore have been 
issued in 66 cases, and correspondence was ongoing with taxpayers in 140 cases 
involving ₹198.78 crore. The Department has not responded to 207 cases of deviations, 
which has an identified risk exposure of ₹569.67 crore. 
Detailed audit of GST returns also suggested significant non-compliance. At the outset, 
essential records such as financial statements and invoices were not produced in 
29 cases out of a sample of 50 taxpayers and in another 21 cases, partial records were 
produced, which constituted a significant scope limitation. Out of the 50 cases that were 
audited either fully or partially due to non/ partial production of records, Audit observed 
19 compliance deficiencies with a revenue implication of ₹690.28  crore. Th e main 
causative factors were availing of ineligible and irregular ITC, exclusion of supplies for 
taxation and incorrect discharge of tax under RCM. 
The Department may:  
1. Establish a monitoring mechanism for prompt follow up of notices issued 
for non-filing and late-filing of GST returns and maintaining proper records 
of compliance.  
2. Develop and implement a mechanism for tracking cases where DRC-07 has 
been issued. Mandate periodic reporting from field officers on recovery 
progress and enforce timely initiation of recovery proceedings under Section 
79 in cases of non-compliance.  
3. Monitor and enforce filing of Form ITC -03 by taxpayers opting for the 
Composition Scheme. Circles must be sensitised to identify such cases and 
ensuring reversal of ITC within 180 days as mandated. 
4. Urgently pursue the 207 deviations identified through data analysis where 
replies were not furnished. Department may also initiate remedial measures 
for all other mismatches and compliance deficiencies pointed out in this 
report. 
Chapter V - Financial Performance of State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs) 
As on 31 March 2023, there were 30 SPSEs (two Statutory Corporations, 24 Government 
Companies (including three inactive Government Companies) and four Government 
Controlled Other Companies) in the State. The contribution of SPSEs to the GSDP of 
Himachal Pradesh shows a declining trend from 2020 -21 to 2022 -23 and reduced 
marginally from 5.47 per cent in 2021-22 to 5.45 per cent in 2022-23. Thirteen SPSEs 
reported profit as per their latest financial statements in 2022 -23 against 10 SPSEs in 
2021-22. The profit decreased from ₹21.47 crore in 2021-22 to ₹20.21 crore in 2022-23. 
The top three SPSEs which contributed maximum profit included Himachal Pradesh 

Overview 
xi 
State Industrial Development Corporation Limited, Himachal Pradesh General 
Industries Corporation Limited and Himachal Pradesh Horticultural Produce Marketing 
and Processing Corporation Limited.  Out of 13 SPSEs which earned profit during 
2022-23, only one SPSE (Himachal Pradesh General Industries Corporation Limited) 
declared dividend of ₹ 0.36 crore. Four SPSEs were not required to declare dividend 
and eight SPSEs which earned aggregate profit of ₹14.88 crore had not declared/paid 
dividend.  
The losses incurred by the SPSEs increased to ₹604.94 crore in 2022-23 (eight SPSEs) 
as per their latest finalised accounts from ₹498.55 crore in 2021-22 (12 SPSEs) due to 
loss-incurring power sector SPSEs (Himachal Pradesh Power Corporation Limited, 
Himachal Pradesh Power Transmission Corporation Limited and Himachal Pradesh 
State Electricity Board Limited) and Himachal Road Transport Corporation. As of 
31 March 2023, 14 SPSEs had accumulated losses amounting to ₹4,985.18 crore. Of 
these, the net worth of nine SPSEs had been fully eroded, resulting in negative net worth.  
As of 30 September 2023, accounts for the year 2022-23 were due from 27 Government 
Companies. However, none of the Government Companies submitted their accounts for 
the year 2022-23 for audit by CAG by 30 September 2023. Seventy three accounts of 
27 Government Companies were in arrears. As on 30 September 2023, two accounts of 
two Statutory Corporations were pending.  
The certified accounts of the selected SPSEs along with the report of the Statutory 
Auditors are reviewed by C&AG by carrying out a supplementary audit. As a result of 
the Supplementary Audit conducted in 19 SPSEs during October 2022 and 
30 September 2023, a number of quantitative as well as qualitative changes were made 
by the SPSEs in their financial statements which led to an improvement in the quality 
of their financial statements. The net financial impact as a result of the Supplementary 
Audit was to t he tune of ₹150.76 crore on the profitability and ₹294.74 crore on the 
assets/liabilities. 
Out of 30 SPSEs, 12 SPSEs are registered as public. Of these, eight SPSEs were required 
to appoint Independent Directors. Only three SPSEs appointed required number of 
Independent Directors, while the remaining five did not appoint Independent Directors. 
Only three SPSEs (out of 25) had conducted the required minimum number of Board of 
Director’s meetings during 2020-23. Out of 24 SPSEs which were required to conduct 
Annual General Meeting (AGM), three SPSEs did not conduct any AGM during 2020-23. 
Out of 12 Public SPSEs, seven SPSEs were required to constitute an Audit Committee, 
however, only three SPSEs constituted Audit Committee . Of these three  SPSEs, the 
criteria of two -third Independent Directors  in the Audit Committee was not met by 
Himachal Pradesh State Industrial Development Corporation Limited and  Himachal 
Pradesh State Electricity Board Limited. 
Out of 12 Public SPSEs, seven SPSEs were required to constitute a Nomination and 
Remuneration Committee as per the criteria specified in Act/Rules. However, only one 
SPSE (Himachal Pradesh State Electricity Board Limited ) constituted this committee. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
xii 
Out of the 25 SPSEs, only four SPSEs established a whistle blower mechanism as 
required by the Companies Act, 2013. 
All the five SPSEs, which were required to constitute Corporate Social Responsibility 
(CSR) committee, had constituted CSR Committees and framed CSR policies. 
However, Himachal Pradesh Power Corporation Limited did not specify the monitoring 
mechanism in its CSR policy. All the three SPSEs (other than power sector) had not 
disclosed their CSR Policy, CSR Committee composition and approved CSR projects 
on their official website, as required by the Companies Act, 2013 . Further, these State 
PSEs did not prepare any annual CSR plan and budget for CSR activities.

Chapter I - General 
 
1.1 Introduction 
This Audit Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG) relates to 
matters arising from Audit of two departments and also includes review of Financial 
Performance of State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs) . A brief introduction of these 
two departments is detailed in the following paragraphs: 
Finance Department 
The Finance Department deals with all matters related to finance in the State which 
includes State Budget, Institutional Finance, Pay Revision, Regulations, Expenditure 
and Pension. The Finance Department acts as a guide to various departments in financial 
matters and is responsible for financial allocations to departments and their monitoring 
and review.  
State Taxes and Excise Department 
The State Taxes and Excise  Department is the major revenue collecting agency in the 
State. The revenue is collected in the shape of various levies such as Taxes, Excise 
Duties and fees etc. under the various Taxation and Excise Acts enforced by this 
department. State Goods and Servi ce Tax, (Major Head/MH 0006) and State Excise  
Duty (MH 0039) are major revenue generating heads. 
1.2 Trend of revenue receipts 
The tax and non-tax revenue raised by the Government of Himachal Pradesh during the 
year 2022-23, the State’s share of net proceeds of divisible Union taxes and duties 
assigned to the State and Grants-in-Aid received from the Government of India during 
the year and the corresponding figures for the preceding four years are depicted in 
Chart 1.1.

Chapter I: General 
3 | P a g e  
The tax revenue increased by ₹ 3,019.65 crore (40 per cent) during the years 20 18-19 
to 2022-23, with an average rate of growth of 8.98 per cent. The rate of growth for 
2022-23 was 9.07 per cent due to rise in SGST (17.34  per cent), State Excise Duty 
(11.90 per cent), Taxes on vehicles (32.38 per cent) and Stamp and Registration Fees 
by 25.16 per cent. There was decrease in taxes on sales, trade etc. by 13.94 per cent and 
in Taxes and duties on electricity by 35.90 per cent. 
The respective Departments reported the following reasons for variations during the 
year: 
• SGST: increased due to higher increase in consumption than anticipated  and 
increased taxpayer compliances and strengthened enforcement.  
• Taxes on Sales, Trade etc.: reduced due to reduced tax rates on petrol and diesel. 
• State Excise: increased due to issuing instructions to district in-charges regarding 
excise collection from time to time and effective implementation of excise policy. 
• Taxes on vehicles : increased due to increased receipts in token tax against the 
target ₹ 97.05 crore, increased vehicle registrations, more issuance of permits, 
better enforcement services etc. 
• Stamp Duty and Registration Fee:  increased due to high registration in deeds  
and there was also increase due to revision in circle rates which are revised from 
1st April every year.

1.7 Response of Government/Departments towards Audit 
The Principal Accountant General (Audit), Himachal Pradesh (PAG), conducts periodic 
inspection of the Government Departments to test check the transactions and verify the 
maintenance of important accounts and other records as prescribed in the rules and 
procedures. These inspections are followed up with Inspection Reports (IRs) 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
8 | P a g e  
incorporating irregularities detected during the inspection and not settled on the spot, 
which are issued to the Heads of the offices inspected with copies to the next higher 
authorities for taking prompt corrective action. 
The heads of the offices are required to comply with the observations contained in the 
IRs within four weeks from the date of receipt of the IRs. Serious financial irregularities 
are reported to the Heads of the Departments/State Government. 
A total of 5,415  audit observations involving ₹ 1,980.53 crore relating to 1,779 
Inspection Reports issued up to March  2023, remained outstanding at the end of 
June 2023 in State Excise and Taxes and Revenue Department.  
Draft audit paragraphs proposed for inclusion in the Audit Report of the Comptroller 
and Auditor General of India are forwarded by the PAG to the Administrative 
Secretaries of the Departments concerned drawing their attention to the audit findings 
and requesting them to send their response within six weeks. The issue of not receiving 
replies from the Departments/ State Government is invariably indicated at the end of 
such paragraphs included in the Audit Report. 
The purpose of audit is to check whether prescribed rules, laws and procedures are being 
adhered to, and to highlight cases of non -compliance, systemic weaknesses, and 
failures. The large number of pending IRs and audit observations pending settlement 
indicate inadequate response to audit observations.  The lack of action on these audit 
observations weakens accountability and raises the risk of loss of revenue.  Increasing 
pendency of audit paragraphs merits urgent attention of the Government for addressing 
the issues consistently raised by Audit. 
1.7.1 Departmental Audit Committee meetings 
The Government had set up Audit Committees under the Chairmanship of the Secretary 
of the concerned Department to monitor and expedite the settlement of audit 
observations included in the IRs. The details of audit committee meetings held during 
the year 2022-23 and the observations settled are depicted in Table 1.7.

Chapter II - Information Technology Audit of e-Procurement in 
Himachal Pradesh 
 
Department of Finance 
 
2.1 Introduction  
e-Procurement is a collaborative procurement of goods and services as well as selection 
of bidders for award of work through use of Internet-based technologies. It ensures 
equal access, fairness, and accountability in procurement process besides  promoting 
competition and shortening the procurement cycle. 
The Government of India (GoI) under Mission Mode Project of National e-Governance 
Plan (NeGP) identified ( June 2009 ) Himachal Pradesh as a pilot State for 
implementation of the e -Procurement. The Government eProcurement System of 
National Informatics Centre , i.e., GePNIC (henceforth referred as e -Procurement 
system) was implemented ( June 2011 ) in three departments  viz., Public Works 
Department (PWD), Irrigation & Public Health Department (IPH) and Industries  
Department/ Controller of Store s on pilot basis. Threshold limit for procurement 
through system was initially fixed (October 2011) at ₹ 40 lakh for PWD and Industries 
Department, and ₹ 25 lakh for IPH which was subsequently reduced (January 2012) to 
₹ 10 lakh (for works) and ₹ 50,000 (for goods and services) for PWD and IPH and 
₹ 20.00 lakh per year for Industries Department. 
In June 2018 the State Government notified that all the departments and corporations/ 
societies/ autonomous/ statutory bodies of the Government of Himachal Pradesh shall 
adopt e -Procurement system for procurement of  goods, services and works with 
estimated value of ₹ five lakh and above. It was also decided that the award of contract 
details shall necessarily be entered in the e -Procurement portal after finali sation of 
tenders. 
Workflow of the e-Procurement system (e-Tendering module) and usage trend 
The workflow of the  e-Tendering module of the e-Procurement system is depicted in 
Chart 2.1. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
12 | P a g e 
Chart 2.1: Workflow of the e-Procurement system 
 
During 2018 -23, 83 departments/ organisations (procuring entities) under the State 
Government were publishing tenders in the e -Procurement portal. Year-wise trend of 
tenders published, value and procuring entities involved during 2018-23 is depicted in 
Chart 2.2. 
Chart 2.2: Trend regarding use of e-Procurement system during 2018-23 
 
2.1.1 Organisational setup 
The State Government designated (August 2010) the  Department of Digital 
Technologies & Governance ( DDT&G, erstwhile Department of Information 
Technologies) as the nodal agency  for implementation of e -Procurement system in 
Himachal Pradesh. In December 2018, Department of Treasuries, Accounts & Lotteries 
(DTAL) was designated as nodal agency.

Chapter II: Information Technology Audit of e-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh

A Core Committee under the Chairmanship of the Principal Secretary (IT) and other 
members1 was constituted (September 2011) for implementation of e-Procurement and 
taking policy decisions.2  
Organisational set up for implementation of e-Procurement 
 
“Information Technology (IT) Audit on e -Procurement Project” had been previously 
conducted and included as Paragraph No. 2.4 in CAG’s Audit Report on Social, General 
and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2017 (Report No. 6 of 
2017 to the Government of Himachal Pradesh). The Report was placed in Himachal 
Pradesh Legislative Assembly in April 2018. As of March 2024, this Audit Report has 
not been discussed in the Public Accounts Committee (PAC) of the State Legislature. 
2.1.2 Audit objectives 
The objectives of the Audit were to assess whether: 
• Various modules of e-Procurement system were implemented and business rules 
were mapped into the system; 
• Integrity, accuracy, reliability and security of data/ information were being 
maintained through adequate IT controls; and 
• Intended objectives viz., transparency and increase in efficiency have been 
achieved. 
2.1.3 Audit Criteria 
• Himachal Pradesh Financial Rules, 2009 and instructions/departmental manuals 
issued by the State Government for e-Procurement; 
 
1  Principal Secretary (Finance) or his representative, Principal Secretary (PWD) or his 
representative, Principal Secretary (IPH) or his representative, Controller of Stores, Director, 
Department of IT as Member Secretary, Joint Director/Deputy Director, Dep artment of IT, and 
any other Technical Expert(s) to be co-opted by the committee.  
2  Revision of tendering rules, issue of executive instructions, vetting of standard bidding  
documents, drafting and signing of Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with banks for 
e-Payment, etc. 


Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
14 | P a g e 
• Information Technology Act, 2000 and subsequent amendment, National  
 e-Governance plan, Request for Proposal (RFP) etc.; 
• Guidelines issued by Central Vigilance Commission (CVC)/ Competition 
Commission of India (CCI); 
• e-Procurement guidelines for Standardisation Testing and Quality Certification 
(STQC) issued by the Ministry of Communication and Information Technology, 
GoI; and 
• Guidelines/ instructions issued from time to time for implementation of 
e-Procurement. 
2.1.4 Audit scope, methodology and sampling 
The Information Technology audit of e-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh covering the 
period 2018-23 was conducted from December 2023 to September 2024 with the focus 
on functioning of all the e-Procurement modules based on data dump supplied by the 
State Government. The Audit methodology included: 
• Analysis of e-Procurement database using SQL queries and MS Excel; 
• Analysis of application controls in the e-Procurement portal; and 
• Analysis of documents uploaded in the e -Procurement portal, as well as 
manual documents related to tender processing in the selected units. 
The audit objectives, criteria, scope and methodology were discussed with the Principal 
Secretary (Finance) in an Entry Conference held on 16  October 2023. An Exit 
Conference to discuss the Audit findings with the Special Secretary -cum-Director, 
DTAL was held on 03 June 2025. 
The data for the period 2018-23 involving 77,152 tenders among 83 procuring entities 
in Himachal Pradesh was analysed with reference to the Audit objectives for checking 
adequacy of validation controls, mapping of business rules and tender processing. 
Sampling of units and tenders was done for detailed scrutiny and to corroborate results 
of the data analysis. 
On the basis of irregularities noticed in data analysis, selection of districts, departments, 
units and e -Tenders for detailed scrutiny was done , using IDEA 3 software. Three 
districts (Hamirpur, Mandi and Shimla ; 25 per cent  out of 12 districts ) and nine4 
departments (11 per cent out of 83 departments/ procuring entities using e-Procurement 
system during 2018 -23) were selected using stratified random sampling. Further, 
51 units5 and 783 tenders (eight per cent out of 10,135 tenders involving irregularities 
in selected units) were selected using random sampling (Appendix 2.1). 
 
3  Interactive Data Extraction and Analysis software used for data analysis, sampling, etc. 
4  Departments with hierarchy: HPPWD, HPJSV , HPSEBL, DMER, and Police; and Departments 
lacking hierarchy: Industries, HPSCSC, Food Civil Supplies & Consumer Affairs, and Digital 
Technologies & Governance.  
5  47 out of 175 units in nine selected departments and four additional units to cover all the 
irregularities. 

Chapter II: Information Technology Audit of e-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh 
15 | P a g e 
Irregularities and issues noticed in implementation of e -Procurement system in 
Himachal Pradesh are discussed in succeeding paragraphs. 
Audit findings 
 
2.2  Deficiencies in system development 
 
2.2.1  Implementation of modules 
In the Request for Proposal (RFP) by the Department of Information Technology in 
June 2009 , various modules 6 were envisaged. Further, the State Government while 
reviewing (October 2011) the progress of e-Procurement decided that the system should 
have modules such as Vendor Management, Indent Management, e-Payment, Contract 
Management, e-Auction and Catalogue Management. As per para 6 of implementation 
guidelines, a State Level Core Committee was to meet once every month during initial 
six months of implementation for smooth rollout of e -Procurement. All policy related 
decisions7 were to be taken by the core committee. 
Audit also highlighted the issue regarding non -implementation of various modules, in 
Audit Report – Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 
31 March 2017 (Paragraph No. 2.4). At that time, only one module – e-Tendering was 
functioning.  
The status of implementation of these modules is detailed below: 
2.2.1.1 Non-implemented modules 
i) Indent Management: Indent Management module facilitates the Government 
department users in creation and submission of indent requests for procurement of 
goods and works, supports estimation of costs for works, goods, and aggregation 
of demand for goods.  
Audit noticed that, this module had not been implemented, and all the above 
processes are handled manually and thereafter the tenders are uploaded on the 
system. Due to non -provision of the module, documentation of indents within 
e-Procurement system could not be achieved. 
ii) Catalogue Management: In the catalogue management module, an online 
database with rate contract information is posted online which shall be searchable 
and organised in a structured manner.  
Audit noticed that there was no provision in the software to capture such details in 
searchable and structured manner. Due to non -deployment of the module, 
processing of purchase orders upon the selected rate contracts were processed 
outside the system.  
 
6  Vendor management, Indent management, e -Tendering, Contract management, e -Payment, 
Catalogue management, e-Auction, etc. 
7  Like revision of tendering rules, issue of executive instructions, finalising the Standard Bidding 
Document across line departments, vetting, drafting, and signing of MoU with banks,  etc. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
16 | P a g e 
iii) Contract Management: The processes between issuance of work order/ purchase 
order and completion of work/ deliverable are handled electronically in contract 
management module.  
Audit noticed that the contract management module had not been implemented due 
to which the progress of work, monitoring of performance of the contactors and 
payment of bills to the contractor through e-Procurement system was not ensured. 
2.2.1.2 Partially implemented modules 
i) Vendor Management: Vendor Management module  enables registration of 
vendors with the system and facilitates them to participate in procurement process.  
Audit noticed that although registration of bidders was being done on the system 
to enable them to participate in tendering process, the credentials such as 
manufacturing capacity, past performance, and financial background, etc. were not 
being captured in the system as master data for future reference. 
ii) e-Auction: The e -Auction module facilitates online conducting of ‘reverse 
auctions’ and ‘forward auctions’8.  
Audit noticed that although e-Auction facility was there in the system, however, its 
usage was low as only 17 tenders between January 2023 to March 2023 were 
published by one Department i.e., Himachal Pradesh State Electricity Board 
Limited (HPSEBL) and out of these only two tenders reached the award of contract 
stage. 
iii) e-Payment: The e-Payment functionality facilitates online transfer of funds, both 
inflows and outflows happening during public procurement covering transactions 
such as sale of tender documents, online transfer of earnest money deposit (EMD), 
and making payments to s uppliers/ contractors for purchase of goods and 
completion of works.  
Audit noticed that e -Payment module had been implemented for inflow payment 
of tender fee and EMD by the bidders only and not for outflow payments to 
bidders. 
In this regard, Audit observed that the Core Committee (constituted in September 2011) 
for implementation of e-Procurement, met (17 October 2011) only once, to review the 
progress of rollout under the Chairmanship of Chief Secretary. In the absence of 
adequate meetings, the Core Committee did not discharge  its duties in taking policy 
related decisions as envisaged, resulting in non-implementation of all the modules and 
deficiencies in e-Procurement system. 
In the Exit Conference,the Government stated that post tender payments are processed 
through IFMS, which are not integrated with the e -Procurement system. The Core 
Committee to address implementation of modules would be made functional. National

Informatics Centre ( NIC) confirmed readiness to implement relevant modules after

due to negotiation practices, which contravene CVC guidelines. The State Government 
may customise the system as per State specific requirements and for implementation of 
intended modules. 
Thus, even after lapse of seven years since the previous CAG’s Audit Report was laid 
in Legislature on e-Procurement, the progress was limited to partial implementation of 
three more modules.

revealed the following deficiencies.

2.2.3.1 Prescribed timelines not flagged 
Rule 102 of Himachal Pradesh Financial Rules, 2009 (HPFR) stipulates that minimum 
time to be allowed for submission of bids shall be three weeks from the date of 
publication of the tender notice or availability of the tender documents for sale, 
whichever is later, for procurements having estimated value of ₹ 10 lakh and above. 
Analysis of data for the period 2018 -23 revealed that in 35,255 tenders (92 per cent) 
out of 38,375 tenders, having estimated value of ₹ 10 lakh and above, gap between bid 
opening and bid closing date ranged between five and 20 days only, in contravention of 
the rule ibid. In the remaining 38,777 tenders (out of 77,152), with estimated value 
below ₹ 10 lakh, the gap between bid opening and bid closing date was less than 
21 days in 36,992 tenders (95 per cent). 
Audit observed that  the system lacked any built -in control mechanism, such as 
automatic red-flag alerts, warnings, or disclaimers, to notify procuring officers when 
tender timelines were set below the permissible limit reflecting inadequate system 
design in terms of compliance assurance. 
This raises concerns whether bidders, particularly new or remote participants, were 
given adequate time to prepare and submit competitive bids, potentially affecting 
competition and equal opportunity in public procurement. 
2.2.3.2 Department-specific timelines not flagged 
Rule 110 of the HPFR, 2009 provides for departments to specify appropriate timelines 
for each stage of procurement to avoid delays. Accordingly, Himachal Pradesh Public 
Works Department ( HPPWD) and Himachal Pradesh Jal Shakti Vibhag ( HPJSV) 
notified (July 2018 and October 2018) 51 days tendering cycle from publication to 
award of work. Similarly, Himachal Pradesh State Electricity Board Limited (HPSEBL) 
notified (September 2020) 45 days tendering cycle. However, no uniform or State-wide 
tender cycle timelines were framed by the State Government or other departments. 
Details regarding tenders published and awarded by these three departments and other 
departments during 2018-23 are given in Table 2.1. 
Table 2.1: Number of tenders published and awarded during 2018-23 
Department Tenders

Tenders reaching 
award stage (per cent) 
Tenders awarded after

HPSEBL 2,122 1,236 (58) 532 (43) 
Other departments 31,498 5,368 (17) No timelines fixed 
Total 77,152 22,006 (28)  
The above analysis clearly indicates that department -specific procurement timelines 
(51 days by HPPWD and JSV , and 45 days by HPSEBL) were not mapped into the 
e-Procurement system. As a result, the system did not trigger alerts or enforce 
compliance when these time  frames were breached, allowing delays in the award of 
contracts to go unchecked.

In the Exit Conference the Government stated that flagging of breach of timelines 
would be considered in consultation with NIC. 
2.2.3.3 Business rules regarding number of minimum bids not mapped 
As per the procurement guidelines (October 2013) issued by the Controller of Stores, 
Himachal Pradesh , a minimum of three bids must be received under the advertised 
tender system. If fewer bids are received, the tender should normally be rejected, and 
re-tendering initiated. In urgent cases, the matter may be referred to the authority next 
above the indenting officer, who may recommend proceeding with the purchase. 
Data analysis of 77,152 tenders published during 2018 -23 revealed that bids ranging 
between one and 99 were received against 62,266 tenders (81 per cent). No bids were 
received against 14,886 tenders (19 per cent). Of these 62,266 tenders, 22,006 reached 
award of contract stage as depicted in Chart 2.3. 
Chart 2.3: Tenders receiving bids during 2018-23 and their award of contract status 
 
From the above, it can be seen that 5,189 tenders receiving less than prescribed three 
bids had reached the Award of Contract (AoC) stage. The e-Procurement system does 
not have any automated checks or red flagging for tenders receiving fewer than three 
bids. No prompts or mandatory fields are present to capture justifications or approvals 
from higher authorities.  
Further, the significant number of less than three bid  tenders reaching the AoC stage 
suggests bypassing of rules and raises concerns about process transparency and 
fairness. Thus, the system lacks capabilities such as auto -detection of non -compliant 
bid counts, and logging of exceptions which are critical for governance, audit trail, and 
transparency. 
The absence of automated red -flagging in the e -Procurement system has allowed 
tenders to be awarded in violation of instructions leading to  failure in enforcing the 
minimum bids. Strengthening the system with compliance checks, red flags, and alerts 
is essential for transparency, competition, and accountability in procurement. 
The above was validated in the physical scrutiny of records in respect of 162 selected 
tenders receiving less than three  bids and reaching AoC stage . It  revealed that in 
87 tenders, approval from higher authority was not found on record  and in the 
remaining 75 tenders, approval was accorded outside the system.

2.2.3.4  Lack of provision in the system to capture rate contract details 
As per Rules 94, 95, and 99 of the HPFR 2009, the Controller of Stores shall conclude 
rate contracts with registered suppliers for commonly used, standard goods needed on 
a recurring basis. The prices and terms of purchase must not exceed or deviate from 
those specified in the rate contract. 
Audit noticed that the e -Procurement software lack ed provisions to capture essential 
rate contract details such as item name, item description, rates, and contractor details. 
These fields were not searchable or organised in a structured format. After rate contracts 
were finali sed, the details were communicated to procuring entities offline, and 
purchase orders were placed manually. The system d id not support automated order 
placement against selected rate contracts. 
In the Exit Conference the Government stated that the observed shortcomings pertain 
to the post-tendering stage and would be addressed through the implementation of the 
Contract Management module, which is yet to be integrated. 
2.2.4  Inadequate controls in the system 
In order to ensure data integrity, suitable controls must be there in the system. Audit 
analysed the data for sufficiency of required controls to check errors.  
2.2.4.1 Strengthening input controls to safeguard e-Procurement integrity 
(i)  Tender fee with zero value: As per notification (October 2013) of the Directorate 
of Industries , Store Purchase Organisation , Himachal Pradesh, suppliers are 
required to pay a fixed tender fee, which is non-refundable. 
Data analysis revealed that the tender fee column ha d zero values in 408 out of 
77,152 tenders published during 2018-23, even though no discounts were allowed 
in the tender fee discount column. The procuring entities had declared zero value 
in the tender fee column at the time of tender creation. This is indicative of the fact 
that the system was lacking control to ensure valid non-zero tender fee value where 
discount on tender fee was not allowed. 
(ii) EMD value entered as ‘zero’ : Rule 106 of HPFR, 2009  stipulates that earnest 
money deposit (EMD) shall be obtained (between two per cent to five per cent of 
the estimated value) from the tenderer(s), to be furnished along with the tender in 
the shape of account payee bank draft or duly pledged fixed deposit receipt. 
Data analysis revealed that in 314 out of 77,152 tenders published during 2018-23, 
EMD value was showing as zero, in spite of the fact that exemption of EMD was 
not allowed in these tenders. This is indicative of the fact that the system lacked 
control to ensure that only valid ‘non-zero’ values are allowed for EMD, if EMD 
discount is not given.  
(iii) Absence of input controls in mandatory fields: Scrutiny of the registered users’ 
data revealed that there were 43,929 registered users11. Of these, 27,765 users were

undermine financial safeguards and user authentication, risking revenue loss and

and generated data.

The system lets bids for the same tender be opened on different days instead of all at 
once on the set date. Opening bids for the same tender on different dates instead of the 
scheduled date violates standard procurement procedures and poses a significant risk 
of unfair advantage or manipulation, particularly if financial bids are accessed 
prematurely. By locking decryption until the official opening time and recording every 
attempt, the platform can ensure a fair, transparent process. 
2.2.4.3  Ensuring gap-free row ID sequencing to safeguard data integrity 
All tables in the e-Procurement database contain a system-generated column titled ‘ID’, 
which serves as the primary field. Each record is automatically assigned a unique 
sequential number by the system, ensuring no duplication of IDs. 
An analysis of these system -generated IDs, however, revealed  gaps in numeric 
sequencing across multiple tables, as detailed in Table-2.4. This indicates that certain 
system-generated serial numbers are missing, which should not occur in an automated 
sequence.

The missing gaps in system -generated Row IDs suggest possible record loss or 
tampering, seriously undermining the integrity and reliability of the e -Procurement 
database. Without a timestamped audit trail to explain these omissions, data 
trustworthiness cannot be established. Enforcing continuous, gap -free sequencing and 
maintaining detailed, timestamped logs for every ID assignment will restore 
transparency and ensure the system’s data remains accurate and verifiable. Audit asked 
for the details regarding audit trail of missing sequences. However, the same were not 
provided. 
2.2.4.4  Automating tender status updates to safeguard transparency 
Tender processing in the e-Procurement system involved human interference in various 
stages viz., tender evaluation, negotiation and award of contract, etc. instead of system 
based evaluation by capturing data inputs given by the authorised users.  
Audit observed that various crucial entries in the system were being done manually by 
the department users, instead of automatic capturing in the e -Procurement system. 
Screenshot depicting that crucial entries were being done manually is depicted in 
Screenshot 2.2. 
Screenshot 2.2: Entry of crucial data by the department users 
  


Chapter II: Information Technology Audit of e-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh 
25 | P a g e 
In this regard audit noticed that: 
(i) Analysis of data for the period 2018 -23 revealed that in 11,056 tenders 17, 
though the bids were decrypted, the tender status was stored and displayed as 
‘To_be_Opened’. Scrutiny of physical files in respect of eight tenders (out of 783 
selected tenders) in which the status as per data was ‘To_be_Opened’ after decryption 
of bids, revealed that all these eight tenders had been cancelled after opening of the 
bids. This indicated that there was difference in status as per data provided and actual 
status at field level due to absence of provision for updat e of status by the system on 
triggering of a particular event. 
(ii) Analysis revealed that 21,962 financial bids submitted by the bidders against 
6,375 out of 62,266 tenders had been opened. However, bid ranks against these bids 
were not entered in the database and showed “Null” value. This was due to absence of 
control in the system to rank automatically after bids are accepted financially.  
Out of the aforementioned 6,375 cases, scrutiny of physical files of 51 sampled tenders 
revealed that 25 tenders had reached the award of contract stage and 26 tenders were 
cancelled. This indicated that there were variations in actual status and status as per 
data provided due to non-capturing of related events automatically. 
(iii) Audit noticed that crucial entries such as L1, L2,...; technically qualified/ not 
qualified; financially qualified/ not qualified; date of award of work; etc. were entered 
manually by the department users in the system. In the absence of any standardised 
codes for these in the form of drop -down options, etc., there were instances of large 
number of entries for same event in the database such as L1, L -1, 1, Ist, L.1, L1 and 
Lowest, etc. for the lowest bidder. 
(iv) In 812 instances where bids were marked as "Rejected_Finance", the bid ranks 
were recorded in the “bid_rank” column using terms such as L1, L -1, 1, Ist, L.1 and 
Lowest, etc., indicating that these bids were recogni sed as the lowest financial bids. 
Thus, due to manual intervention in update of these crucial entries, there were instances 
of wrong data entries. Audit findings after scrutiny of physical files of 20 sampled 
tenders where the irregularity was noticed are discussed in Paragraph 2.3.3.2. 
Relying on manual updates and free‐text entries ha d led to mismatches between what 
the system shows and what actually happened. Automating status changes and 
standardising bid rankings will keep tender records accurate, transparent, and easy to 
audit. 
In the Exit Conference , the Government stated (June 2025) that system generates 
ranking of bidders automatically, but errors occur during manual entries after 
finalisation of selected bidder. Standardi sed formats such as ‘L followed by numeric’ 
can be introduced along with a mechanism of flagging such discrepancies in the system. 
The system did not generate any automatic bid ranking as per the data made available 
to audit.

2.3  System utilisation 
Audit analysed the level and effectiveness of e -Procurement portal utilisation by the 
procuring entities through data analysis and scrutiny of records. The findings in this 
regard are in the succeeding paragraphs. 
2.3.1 Partial adoption of e-Procurement system by State procuring agencies 
Data analysis revealed that , as of March 2023, there were 100 procuring entities/ 
organisations which were registered in the e -Procurement system. However, 
84 procuring entities utilised the system and published 1.05 lakh tenders with total 
value of ₹ 80,984 crore since inception (June 2011). During the Audit period (2018-23), 
83 entities published 0.77 lakh tenders through the system, amounting to a total value 
of ₹ 66,265.30 crore.  
Audit noticed that despite instructions of the State Government in June 2018 ibid, only 
83 out of the 100 registered procuring entities published tenders through 
e-Procurement. Details are given below (Appendix 2.2). 
• 16 out of 100 procuring entities/ organisations such as Dr. Rajendra Prasad 
Government Medical College Tanda, Directorate of Youth Services and 
Sports, Himachal Pradesh Vidhan Sabha, Himachal Pradesh Institute of Public 
Administration ( HIPA), etc.  did not use the e -Procurement portal since 
inception as of March 2023. 
• 15 of the 83 procuring entities/ organisations, which published tenders during 
2018-23, such as Himachal Pradesh Technical University Hamirpur, Himachal 
Pradesh Public Service Commission, Directorate of Higher Education, 
Directorate of Health Safety and Regulation, etc., had utilised the portal for a 
period of less than one year and published tenders ranging between one and 
nine only.  
Despite the State Government’s clear directive in June 2018, several procuring entities 
had either never used or only minimally engaged with the e -Procurement portal, 
compromising its goals of transparency and efficiency. With 16 entities yet to publish 
a single tender and 15 others publishing fewer than ten contracts, the system’s full 
potential remains untapped. Prompt corrective measures , renewed mandates, targeted 
support, and regular compliance monitoring  are essential to ensure all entities adopt 
e-Procurement and uphold competitive, transparent procurement across the State. 
In the Exit Conference , the Government stated (June 2025) that matter regarding 
non-utilistion of system by some entities would be looked into. 
2.3.2  Updation of tender processing stages 
e-Procurement workflow envisages a sequential step-by-step progress through various 
stages of a tender, culminating in Award of Contract (AoC) to the successful bidder. 
Thus, reaching the AoC stage in the tender process is an indicator of complete and 
effective use of e-Procurement portal. The e-Procurement portal provides for uploading 
of summary reports at each stage of the tender process, as detailed in Table 2.5:

B. In the e-Procurement system, the next stage in the tendering process should have 
been enabled/ accessed only after completion of the previous stage i.e., after uploading 
of summary report of the previous stage. Data analysis showed that technical evaluation 
summary of 13 tenders  and financial evaluation summary of two tenders  were not 
uploaded despite these tenders having entered the subsequent stages (Appendix 2.4).  
This is indicative of the fact that uploading of summary reports was not mandatory and 
tenders could enter the subsequent stage without completion of previous stage by 
resorting to manual interference outside the system. 
Out of the selected 783 tenders, 681 tenders reached the AoC stage as per field 
verification. Of these, technical evaluation summary and financial evaluation summary 
were uploaded in respect of 673 and 606 tenders, respectively. It was noticed that: 
• Technical summary reports of 16 out of 673 tenders were uploaded after date of 
actual award of contract. 
• Financial summary reports of 384 out of 606 tenders were uploaded after date of 
actual award of contract. 
Uploading of financial and technical summary reports , after actual award of contract 
meant that actual sequence of events in tendering process was not adhered to. This was 
due to absence of application control in the system to ensure the sequence of uploading 
of the summary reports , affecting transparency and objectivity in the tendering process 
and dependence on manual records. 
No response in this regard was furnished (April/ May 2025).

D. As per the project proposal (Para 6.1), a Project Management Unit (PMU) was 
to be established under NIC to facilitate training, manpower support, bidder assistance, 
and handholding. Additionally, Para 3.4 required the creation of a demonstration site 
for hands-on training. 
Audit scrutiny revealed that ₹ 198.70 lakh was released (October and November 2010) 
to NIC Himachal Pradesh, including ₹  63.50 lakh earmarked for training/awareness. 
Although NIC informed the Department in January 2011 about the availability of a 
demonstration site (http://164.100.72.138/nicgep/app), the site was found 
non-functional as of December 2024. 
Training was limited to initial phase in December 2011, where 81 bidders and 220 
department officials were trained. No records were available for training activities 
during 2018–2023. Furthermore, NIC had not submitted Utilisation Certificates (UCs) 
for the funds earmarked for training. 
Despite registration of 100 procuring entities on the e -Procurement system, training 
remained confined to only three pilot departments, indicating inadequate oversight by 
the Core Committee.  
In the Exit Conference , the Government replied (June 2025) that instructions were 
issued from time to time for updating the tender stage s in the system. The system 
recorded the date of uploading of the award of contract instead of the actual date of 
award. It was further stated that cases with very little time in uploading the summary 
reports would be scrutinised and for delay in uploading of summary reports directions 
would be issued. 
The State Government may issue directions for real time updation of tender stages and 
timely uploading the summary reports. Further, system generated alerts/ red flags for 
delays may be introduced.

2.3.3  Tender Processing 
In the e -Procurement system, t he bidders by using their Digital Signature Certificate 
(DSCs), submit the bid online and upload the documents, and the department users 
having bid opening roles, open/ decrypt the bids with their DSCs and other credentials. 
Once the comparative statement is generated, the successful bidder is called for 
negotiation or sample checking. The last stage is award of contract where the 
department user uploads the award of contract mentioning the contract/ award value, 
finalised with the selected bidder. Deficiencies noticed in tender processing are detailed 
in the succeeding paragraphs. 
2.3.3.1  Award of tender after bid validity expiry 
Every tender specifies a bid validity period within which the contract must be awarded. 
Audit observed that during 2018–23, 71 per cent of tenders (55,146 out of 77,152) had 
no record of award details on the e -Procurement portal. Of the remaining 22,006 
tenders, 9,050 were awarded after expiry20 of the bid validity period. 
Delay in award of 9,050 tenders (41 per cent) after bid expiry period indicated that even 
after adoption of e -Procurement system there were delays in award of most of the 
tenders. The e-Procurement system lacked the provision to promptly flag instances of 
inordinate delays at various stages of tende ring process. System did not ensure 
processing of tenders with prescribed timelines, besides, critical activities like technical 
evaluation, financial evaluation, negotiation and award of contract were done manually 
outside the system. 
In the Exit Conference, the Government stated (June 2025) that the date on which the 
award of contract is uploaded is captured in the system, instead of actual date of award. 
Suitable directions for timely uploading of award of contracts would be issued. 
2.3.3.2  Not awarding tender to L1 after opening of financial bids 
Rule 136 of HPFR, 2009 regarding transparency, competition, fairness and elimination 
of arbitrariness in the procurement process states that negotiation with bidders after bid 
opening must be severely discouraged. However, in exceptional circumstances wher e 
price negotiation is necessary due to some unavoidable circumstances, the same may 
be resorted to only with the lowest evaluated responsive bidder. Further, Central 
Vigilance Commission instructed (March 2007) that generally there would be no 
negotiation with bidders. In special circumstances, if it is to be done, it should be done 
with the lowest bidder only. 
Analysis of data in respect of 62,266 tenders, where bids were received during 2018-23 
revealed that in 812 cases, tenders were not awarded to the L1 bidder after opening of 
the financial bids and system generated BoQ statements. Audit verified physical files 
of 20 sampled tenders (Appendix 2.5) and it was noticed that: 
 
20  After bid validity expiry date:– Up to three months: 4,939; four to six months: 1,929; seven to 12 
months: 1,523; 13 to 24 months: 595; 25 to 36 months: 50; and more than 36 months: 14.

to instances of subjective selection of successful bidders. This had defeated the very 
purpose of improving transparency in decision making in public procurement and 
reducing the scope of subjectivity. 
In the Exit Conference, the Government stated (June 2025) that such instances would 
be investigated and required functionalities would be incorporated in the system.

The tender committee for the purpose of evaluation of financial bids uses the system 
generated comparative BoQ statement for deciding L1 bidder. After acceptance of the 
AoC, award details are entered in the e -Procurement system by the procuring entity 
manually. Screenshot of the portal confirming that these details are entered manually 
in the system is depicted in Screenshot 2.3.

In another tender (2021_HPSEB_43909_1), all the bidders while submitting

entity, while awarding the contract to L1, prepared the financial evaluation 
summary manually, including GST amount.

reliability issues in the system. Functionality in the system and customi sation 
of BoQs to deal with such cases through the system should be there. 
• In one tender (2018_UDDHP_20234_1) , after opening of financial bids, L1 
refused to carry out the work at quoted rates (₹ 1.84 lakh), for which his EMD

violation of CVC guidelines and awarded ( ₹ 2.91 lakh ) the contract to L2. 
Further, instead of entering the name of selected contractor (L2) in the system, 
the name of L1 was entered, raising concerns with data reliability. 
Above instances may be attributed to wrong data entry of awarded amount, issues in 
system generated BoQ summary, award of contract at higher prices than quoted 
amount, etc. being done manually outside the system. 
Thus, due to manual intervention in entering details of award of contract and data entry 
errors, besides, lack of functionalities in the system to deal with various cases as 
detailed above, selection of successful bidder involved increased human intervention . 
This was not in conformity with the aim of increasing the efficiency and transparency 
in procurement process. 
2.3.4 Other deficiencies 
2.3.4.1  Discrepancies in use of Digital Signature Certificate 
Rule 9.1.5 of guidelines for usage of Digital Signatures23 in e-Governance, 2010 states 
that the digital signature verification process is same for certificates issued to all 
categories of users. Each user in the e-Procurement system has to register with an e-mail 
ID as his/ her user ID along with a valid DSC to perform activities in tendering process. 
Data analysis revealed that 36,234 DSCs were mapped against 12,778 user IDs ranging 
between two and 15 DSCs per user ID. Of these, 8,5 66 users participated in 57,1 18 
tenders involving 1,87,928 bids during 2018-23. The following issues were noticed: 
• The name of the user was not validated at the time of renewal of DSC and 
different names were captured against the same user ID in 6,706 cases. For 
instance, eight distinct alias names were mapped with user ID “3176” and five 
distinct alias names were mapped with user ID “6268”. 
 
23  Digital Signature Certificates (DSC)  are the digital equivalent (that is electronic format) of 
physical or paper certificates. A digital certificate can be presented electronically to prove your 
identity, to access information or services on the internet or to sign certain documents digitall y. 
Like physical documents are signed manually, electronic documents are required to be signed 
digitally using a DSC. Transactions that are done using internet if signed using a DSC becomes 
legally valid. A licensed Certifying Authority (CA) issues the DSC . CA means a person who has 
been granted a license to issue a DSC under Section 24 of the Indian IT-Act, 2000. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
36 | P a g e 
• In 71 cases, multiple DSCs (ranging between three and eight) were issued to 
single user within a financial year. In one case, 12 DSCs were mapped against 
the same user ID “7277” and six out of these 12 DSCs were mapped within 
six months (from 23 July 2019 to 24 January 2020). In another case, nine DSCs 
were mapped against the same user ID “15529” and five out of these nine DSCs 
were mapped within nine days (from 25 May 2021 to 02 June 2021). 
• In 15 cases, one DSC was mapped with two different user IDs. Of these, in one 
case, one DSC “1552929” was mapped with two user IDs (1794 and 24673) as 
well as with two different alias names. 
The above deficiencies indicated lack of validation control in the system which resulted 
in improper mapping of DSC with the user ID and name due to which legal sanctity of 
DSC and legitimacy of bids was not ensured. 
In the Exit Conference , the Government stated (June 2025) that multiple IDs are 
permissible for operational flexibility, however, possibility of flagging such cases 
would be explored. 
2.3.4.2  Unavailability of information on blocked users  
Analysis of data for user s’ blocked history revealed that users were blocked or 
unblocked due to various reasons24. During 2018-23, the dataset contained 87 records 
pertaining to 81 user IDs that had been blocked at some point. It was however, noticed 
that, several mandatory fields — such as blocked period, authorised person doing 
blocking/ unblocking, reasons, etc. were either left bl ank or contained ‘NULL’ value 
as given in Table 2.12.

Blocked and unblocked details must be captured by the system for audit trail, in the

and user accountability in the System. 
2.3.4.3  Non-provision for public to view tender documents after document  
download/ sale end date 
The e-Procurement system has provision for the general public to view the tender status 
of each published tender. Tender summary reports containing details and documents 
uploaded at each stage would be available for viewing and downloading by general 
public. 
Audit, however, noticed that these details and documents are not available for public 
viewing and downloading after ‘Document download/ sale end date’. In the absence of 
 
24  Wrong username, User retired, transferred, Login id no longer in use, on request, etc.

which, public interested in tracking the works or comparing the outcome with the 
tender specifications are unable to do so, defeating the objective of transparency. 
2.4 Potential Collusive Bidding 
Tender process is intended to enable procurement on the most favourable terms and 
conditions. This intention is defeated if, bidders collude or act in agreement by forming 
cartels25. Such collusive bidding or bid rigging 26 counteracts the purpose of tendering 
and is inherently anti-competitive. 
Data analysis for the period 2018 -23 revealed various patterns of potential collusive 
bidding viz., bid rotation, bid submission and award from same IP address, submission 
of bids for a tender from same IP address, etc. Audit findings in this regard are discussed 
in succeeding paragraphs. 
2.4.1  Bidding from same IP address 
Internet Protocol (IP) address is a series of numbers that identifies any device on a 
network. Computers use IP addresses to communicate with each other, both over the 
internet, as well as on other networks. Placing more than one bid for a tender from the 
same IP address shows that the same computer was used to place the bids and is an 
indicator of potential collusion among bidders. Further, there is likelihood of collusion 
between bidders and department users , if bids are placed and subsequently awarded 
from same IP address or from premises of the procuring entity , compromising data 
secrecy and confidentiality. 
The issue of bidding from same IP address was raised in the CAG’ s Audit Report laid 
in the Legislature in April 2018 as well.  In the Exit Conference (June 2025), t he 
Government accepted the issue and stated that such cases would be reviewed, and 
necessary instructions would be issued. Further, the possibility of flagging such cases 
by the system itself would be explored. 
2.4.1.1 Procurement integrity at risk: Bids submitted from procuring entity IP 
addresses 
Analysis of data for the period 2018 -23, revealed that in case of 281 tenders  (out of 
22,006 awarded tenders)  bidding and subsequent award of contract (AoC) was done 
from the same IP address. Of these, in 41 tenders all the bids (between two and four ) 
were submitted from IP address of the procuring entity, as the same IP address was later 
used for award of the contracts. Some illustrations are given below: 
 
25  ‘Cartel’ includes an association of producers, sellers, distributors, traders or service providers who, 
by agreement amongst themselves, limit, control or attempt to control the production, distribution, 
sale or price of or trade in goods or provision of s ervices (explanation to Section 3(3) of the 
Competition Act, 2002). 
26  ‘Bid rigging’ is defined as any agreement, between enterprises or persons engaged in identical or 
similar production or trading of goods or provision of services, which has the effect of eliminating 
or reducing competition for bids or adversely affecting o r manipulating the process for bidding 
(as defined in Section 2(c)of the Competition Act, 2002). 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
38 | P a g e 
• In the office of Director, Urban Development, Himachal Pradesh, an IP address 
(103:210:32:152) was used for award of 12 tenders during 2018 -23 to the L1 
bidder, who had uploaded his/ her bids from the same IP address. 
• In the selected units, bidding by the L1 bidder and subsequent award of contract 
from the same IP address was noticed in 60 tenders. Of these, in 17 tenders 
(receiving two bids), both bids were uploaded from the IP address from which 
award of contracts was subsequently done as detailed in Appendix 2.6. 
It was further noticed  that bidders had submitted bids from the IP address of the 
procuring entity within very short time gap on same day, with little margin between 
their quoted prices. In most of the above cases, same set of bidders had participated in 
tenders receiving bids from same IP address and on same date.  Apart from this , 
procuring entity’s IP address was used by the bidders beyond office hours. 
The fact that in 41 tenders all bids and the award of contract originated from the 
procuring entity’s own IP address often mere minutes apart and outside normal office 
hours strongly suggests potential collusion or insider facilitation. This practice 
fundamentally undermines the fairness, transparency, and competitive integrity of the 
procurement process.  
2.4.1.2 Bidding by different bidders from same IP address 
Submission of bids for a tender from the same IP address by different bidders within a 
short span of time is indicative of potential collusion amongst bidders. 
Analysis of data for the period 2018-23 revealed that in 30,731 tenders (49 per cent of 
tenders against which bids were received), multiple bids27 were received from same IP 
address. Of these, in 5,177 tenders all bids ranging between two and seven were 
received from same IP address. It was further noticed that: 
• Multiple bids were submitted from same IP address in 3,174 tenders, 184 
tenders and four tenders within  short timespan of  one hour, five minutes and 
one minute respectively; one of the bidders , who had submitted  bid from the 
same IP address, becoming L1. 
• Multiple bids were submitted from same IP address by the bidders within short 
timespan also on the last day of bid submission: 
o In 1292 tenders, the timespan was one hour (435 in last hour  of the bid 
submission end time as well). 
o In 91 tenders, the timespan was five minutes (six in last five minutes  as 
well). 
o In four tenders, the timespan was one minute (three in last minute as well).  
 
 
27  Tenders received bids between two and 38. 

Chapter II: Information Technology Audit of e-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh 
39 | P a g e 
Potential collusive behaviour – analysis of tenders receiving two bids 
Audit analysed the data of tenders receiving two bids during 2018-23. It was noticed 
that out of the 14,085 tenders (receiving two bids), in 1,461 tenders, both the bids were 
submitted from same IP address.  This raises a significant red flag regarding the 
independence and competitiveness of the bidding process. Further, a total of 771 unique 
bidder pairs were observed to have participated together in tenders. Out of these, 10 
specific bidder pairs participated together in 10 or more tenders. Collectively, these top 
10 pairs participated in 206 tenders (14 per cent of these tenders), suggesting a pattern 
of repeated and coordinated bidding behaviour. Such frequent collaborations indicated 
potential cartelisation or lack of genuine competition. Further analysis of these 10 pair 
of bidders revealed that: 
• Among four pairs, tenders were consistently awarded to one member of the pair . 
For instance, in the pair with user IDs 1598–13144, all 32 tenders were awarded to 
user ID 13144. Similarly, in pair  with user IDs 576–1380, all 23 tenders were 
awarded to user ID 1380. Such patterns suggest potential coordination or an unfair 
advantage, especially when the same bidder is always awarded the tender despite 
joint participation. 
• In 76 tenders, L1 bidder was always first bidder. This suggests a pattern where the 
winning bidder consistently submits bids first, which could indicate pre -planned 
submissions or access advantage. 
• In 12 tenders, L1 bidder was always second bidder to participate. This could point 
to a manipulated bidding order to make the process appear competitive. 
• In 14 tenders, both bidders had same mobile number, which was a clear signal of 
shared identity or coordination.

The e-Procurement data from 2018–23 revealed a clear pattern of bid collusion: nearly 
half of all tenders received multiple bids from the same IP address within minutes, with 
winning bids frequently appeared to be pre-determined. Such practices undermine d 
competitive pricing and transparency, leading to award rates consistently above 
estimates. Further, the system lacked controls to identify and flag suspected collusion , 
and evaluation of bids was not done with due diligence.

2.4.2  Splitting up of work leading to circumvention of e-Tendering 
As per instructions (April 2012) of the E ngineer-in-Chief, HPPWD, no efforts should 
be made to split up the big projects into smaller jobs either to avoid e -Tendering or to 
avoid wide publication and approval of higher authorities. Audit noticed that: 
(A) In HPPWD division Sundernag ar, splitting of works29 into smaller jobs with 
estimated cost less than ₹ five lakh, to avoid e-Tendering was noticed. Details are given 
in Table 2.17.

The possibility of bid rigging is high when competing bidders have a family 
relationship, as one bidder would be able to influence the other bidder. Bidding for 
same tenders by the bidders having family relationships viz. husband and wife was 
noticed in 20 tenders  published between October 2019 and July 2020 in HPPWD 
Electrical division, Mandi. Data analysis and scrutiny of records further revealed that: 
• Two bids were received in all the 20 tenders from the same IP address. 
• Three usernames were used to submit the bids – one by husband and two by wife. 
• Bids in 18 out of the 20 tenders, were submitted on same day each. Of which,
