It can be seen from the above that circumvention of e -Tendering had led to lack of 
competition resulting in award of works at much higher prices. Participation of same 
set of bidders and final award of respective jobs to one bidder only indicated potential 
collusion amongst bidders. Thus, either estimation of works was faulty or kept below 
₹ five lakh for each job deliberately, to avoid e-Tendering. 
(B) Analysis of information received from 11 divisions of HPPWD and 19 divisions of 
HPJSV in respect of 1916 tenders  with estimated cost between ₹ four lakh and 
₹ five lakh (processed outside system) revealed that 1812 tenders were awarded. Of 
these, 551 (30 per cent) tenders 30 were awarded above ₹ five lakh, while 10 tenders 
were awarded above ₹ 10 lakh. 
The deliberate fragmentation of high -value contracts into sub -threshold jobs ha d 
systematically undermined e -tendering, stifled competition and transparency, and 
driven up costs by nearly 88 per cent in the Sundernagar case alone. To restore value 
for money and deter collusion, it is imperative to enforce single -package electronic 
tendering for all works above ₹ five lakh, integrate automated checks to flag cumulative 
contract values, and institute regular audits of estimate -to-award patterns. These 
measures will reinforce competitive integrity and curb financial inefficiencies across 
HPPWD and HPJSV divisions. 
2.5 Conclusion 
The e-Procurement system launched by the State Government in June 2011 remains 
partially implemented, hampering the intended transparency, efficiency, and 
accountability in public procurement. The project was operationalised without a 
formal Service Level Agreement (SLA) with NIC, and inadequate convening of the 
Core Committee meetings hampered oversight and timely course correction during 
rollout. 
Despite the 2018 mandate requiring all procurements above ₹  five lakh to be 
conducted through the e-Procurement portal, system utilisation remains sub-optimal. 
One of the central deficiencies observed was the system’s failure to incorporate 
automated red-flagging mechanisms to detect and escalate anomalies or suspicious 
 
30  HPPWD: 287 out of 724 and HPJSV: 264 out of 1,088. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
44 | P a g e 
activity. This absence of early warning indicators ha d allowed systemic and 
procedural lapses to persist undetected. 
The platform lacked critical functionalities aligned with Himachal Pradesh Financial 
Rules (HPFR) and department -specific timelines, resulting in delays and inefficient 
tender lifecycle management besides lack of red flags to alert such delays. Weak input 
controls had led to data integrity issues, incorrect bid information, decryption failures, 
and incomplete audit trails. 
The objectives of training and support through the PMU were only partially met. The 
training was limited to the initial phase, and no records existed for later years. Absence 
of input controls in mandatory fields led to incomplete user data, as seen in the 
registration of users during 2018 –23 and lack of validation controls led to improper 
DSC-user mapping, compromising the legal sanctity of DSCs and the legitimacy of 
bids. 
Technical deficiencies such as flawed bid evaluation, lack of support for rate 
contracts, and omission of features like GST inclusion further undermine d 
compliance. Involvement of manual interventions in tendering process, including 
inconsistent updating of tender stages and award of contract, had led to procedural 
deviations, such as awarding tenders to non-L1 bidders. 
Critically, the system did not flag or alert authorities to potentially collusive or anti -
competitive behaviour. Audit analytics uncovered strong indicators of bid rigging, 
including bids submitted from procuring entities’ IP addresses or from identical IPs 
within short time span; minimal bid variation and rotational patterns suggestive of 
coordinated submissions; award of contracts at inflated rates; and tender splitting to 
evade e -Tendering. These red flags were neither automatically detected nor 
investigated, reflecting the system’s failure to provide real-time compliance alerts. 
2.6  Recommendations  
Based on the audit findings, to address the systemic, procedural, and technical 
shortcomings of the e-Procurement system, the State Government should: 
R-1. Revitalise the Core Committee with regular meetings, defined roles, and 
clear follow -up protocols to oversee system implementation, policy 
decisions, and issue resolution. 
R-2. Enhance the system to fully support compliance with HPFR and 
department-specific procurement timelines, including auto -tracking and 
alerts for delays. 
R-3. Strengthen input controls to prevent incorrect or incomplete data entries and 
ensure data consistency and integrity across modules. 
R-4. Incorporate real -time updates of tender stages and automated summary 
reports to reduce manual intervention and improve transparency through 
enhanced automation.  

Chapter II: Information Technology Audit of e-Procurement in Himachal Pradesh 
45 | P a g e 
R-5. Conduct regular, training sessions for both procuring entity officials and 
bidders, with a focus on system usage, bid submission, and security 
protocols. Establish a responsive helpdesk to provide technical and 
procedural support to users. 
R-6. Robust input and validation controls should be implemented to ensure 
completeness of user data while registration and proper mapping of Digital 
Signature Certificates ( DSCs) with user IDs, thereby upholding the legal 
sanctity of DSCs and the legitimacy of bids. 
R-7. Develop algorithms to flag potential collusion and suspicious bidding 
patterns such as use of identical IPs, bid timing overlaps, price collusion, 
repetitive award patterns, or unusual bidding behaviour. 
R-8. The threshold limit for e-Tendering may be reduced across all departments 
to promote wider adoption of e-Procurement and prevent tender splitting.

System 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  

47 | P a g e  
Chapter III - Performance Audit on E-Way Bill System 
Department of State Taxes and Excise  
 
3.1 Introduction to Electronic Way Bill (EWB) 
Goods and Services Tax (GST) has been introduced with effect from 1 st July 2017, 
subsuming a wide range of indirect taxes based on the paradigm of ‘One Nation One 
Tax’. One of the intended objectives of GST regime was to improve efficiency in 
movement of goods and services by reducing process-related time delays.  
Way Bill was a feature present even in pre -GST regime, wherein movement of goods 
was administered through manually governed (revenue) check posts. Goods entering a 
particular State was levied an ‘Entry Tax’ which has since been subsumed under GST. 
E-Way Bill ( EWB) is conceived as a shift from Government -monitored tax 
administration model to a self-reporting model by the taxpayer. 
The Government’s key objective behind introduction of EWB is to safeguard revenue. 
EWB is a document required for movement of goods and is designed to capture details 
of goods before being moved. Automation and standardisation of the entire process was 
intended to check tax evasion and shore up GST collections.  EWB is also designed to 
dissolve the no n-trade barriers, so that transit time is reduced and supply chain 
efficiency is improved. 
EWB was introduced with effect from 01 April 2018 for all inter-state movement of 
goods having value exceeding ₹50,000. For the intra-state movements, EWB was made 
mandatory in phases, with the threshold limit as may be prescribed by the concerned 
State Governments. The threshold limit (for inter-state/intra-state movement) for the 
consignment value is ₹50,000 for Himachal Pradesh. 
Rule 138 of Himachal Pradesh Goods and Services Tax Rules ( HPGST Rules), 2017 
(amended from time to time) provides for the EWB mechanism. The information of the 
consignment is to be furnished prior to movement of goods and it is to be issued 
irrespective of whether the movement is in relation to supply or for reasons other than 
supply. 
3.2 Organisational Structure1 of the Department 
The Principal Secretary (State Taxes and Excise)  to the Government of Himachal 
Pradesh is the administrative head of the department. The State Taxes & Excise (ST&E) 
department has two wings 2 i.e., Excise wing and Goods and Service s Tax wing. This 
 
1  Before restructuring (January 2024) of the Department, Commissioner (State Taxes and Excise) 
was head of the Department and worked as Controller of taxpayers across the State. There were 
two Additional Commissioner (ST&E) Gr -I, two Additional Commissioner (ST&E) Gr-II, Joint 
Commissioner, ST&E (D), three Zones and 13 Deputy Commissionerate (ST&E) and three Flying 
squads thereunder ( total 16 units) . All the field functionaries were also entrusted with the 
Preventive/ Enforcement Functions.  
2  Vide Notification No. EXN -B(1)-1/2021 dated 19 th January 2024, the restructuring of the 
Department has been done. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
48 | P a g e  
department is headed by a Commissioner (State Taxes and Excise). The GST wing has 
a three-tier structure comprising Headquarter, Zones and Circles.  There ar e three 
Additional Commissioner (ST&E) Gr-I/II; seven Joint Commissioners, ST&E; three 
GST Z ones; 10 Deputy Commissioner State Taxes & Excise (DCST&E); and 
60 Assistant Commissioner State Taxes & Excise (ACST&E) Circles.  
EWBs (effected for supplies) are typically accounted under the Return GSTR -1 and 
GSTR-4/GST-CMP-08 by the normal and composition taxpayers respectively and are 
required to be verified b y the Assistant Commissioners under each GST Taxpayer 
Service Commissionerate at the time of scrutiny of returns . The Deputy 
Commissionerates are Preventive units/ Enforcement units which perform wider 
anti-evasion duties, including verification of EWBs, headed by the Proper Officers3 i.e., 
Assistant Commissioner of State Taxes & Excise. 
3.3 Information Systems used for EWBs  
The EWB Common portal is managed by National Informatics Centre (NIC). In 
February 2020, EWB portal has been integrated with the VAHAN system of the 
Ministry of Road Transport & Highways (MoRTH), so that vehicle registration number 
can be validated at the time of generating EWB. On the EWB Common Portal, one -
time registration of the taxpayer is required for the purpose of generation, extension, 
cancellation and rejection of EWBs.  
The Proper Officers can access the EWB Portal through two means: - (i) Logging into 
EWB Common Portal through a web browser using the login credentials provided , or 
(ii) Logging into the GST EWB System mobile application. The functions performed 
by the Proper Officer are verification and approval of EWBs, rejection and cancellation 
of EWBs, extension of validity of EWBs, verification of goods in-transit, initiation of 
penalty and legal actions, monitoring and reporting, unblocking of EWBs, viewing and 
accessing Management Information System (MIS) reports etc.  
3.4 Processes involved in the EWB System  
The EWB system includes various processes such as the enrolment of the required 
persons in the portal, generation of EWB, extension, cancellation and rejection of the 
EWBs generated etc. The entire process flow of EWB system under GST is depicted in 
Figure 3.1.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
3  As per section 2 (91) of the HPGST Act 2017 the Proper Officer in relation to any function to be 
performed under this Act, means the Commissioner or the officer of the State tax who is assigned 
that function by the Commissioner.

Figure 3.1 - E-waybill Mechanism – Process Flow Chart

50 | P a g e  
3.4.1 Enrolment in the Portal
 
GST registered persons, transporters (GST Registered or Unregistered) and citizens/ 
unregistered persons are required to enrol themselves in the EWB portal by providing 
necessary details such as State, GSTIN, legal name, PAN, mobile number etc. to 
generate EWB. The transporters, who may not be registered under the GST but cause 
movement of goods for their clients, need to enrol on the EWB portal and get a 15-digit 
Unique Transporter ID. 
3.4.2 Generation of EWBs 
As per Rule 138 of the HPGST Rules, EWB can be generated electronically by 
furnishing details in Part A (once submitted cannot be edited) of the Form GST 
EWB-01 by any of the following persons: 
• the Consignor 
• the Transporter 
➢ in the case of authorisation by the Consignor or 
➢ if the Consignor is an unregistered person 
• the Consignee 
Details of the consignments  viz., GSTIN of supplier, place of dispatch, GSTIN of 
recipient, place of delivery, document number, document date, value of goods, 
Harmonised System of Nomenclature (HSN) code and reason for transportation are 
available in Part-A of the EWB-01 and in Part-B, vehicle number for consignment by 
road and Transport document number are provided.  Furnishing details of vehicle 
number is not mandatory for intra-state/ Union Territory transactions with a distance 
upto 50 km when the goods are moved from premises of consignor to that of transporter 
or from the premises of transporter to that of consignee. Consolidated EWBs can be 
generated in Form GST EWB -02 in the case of multiple EWBs generated for the 
multiple consignments but transported in the single vehicle. 
3.4.3 Bill to - Ship to Transactions
 
Bill to-Ship to supplies, wherein the buyer and the consignee are different persons and 
EWB can be generated either by the buyer or the supplier in these transactions. Buyer 
orders the supplier to supply the goods to the consignee on behalf of the buyer involving 
two supplies and two invoices in the transaction. Central Board of Indirect Taxes & 
Customs (CBIC) has clarified vide press release dated 23  April 2018 that either the 
buyer (consignor) or the seller (supplier) can generate EWB in a Bill to -Ship to  
transaction. Depending on the person (buyer/ supplier) generating the EWB, the fields 
in Part-A of EWB-01 have to be furnished, viz.,  
• EWB is generated by the supplier – Bill From (details of supplier), Dispatch 
From (details of principal/additional place of business of supplier), Bill To 
(details of buyer/  consignor), Ship To (address of recipient/  consignee) and 
Invoice details (raised by supplier to buyer) are to be furnished. 

Chapter III: Performance Audit on E-Way Bill System 
51 | P a g e  
• EWB is generated by the buyer – Bill From (details of buyer/  consignor), 
Dispatch From (Principal/  additional place of business of supplier), Bill to 
(details of recipient/ consignee), Ship to (address of recipient/  consignee), and 
Invoice Details (raised by buyer to recipient) are to be furnished.
 
3.4.4 Validity of EWB 
Validity of the EWB depends upon the distance, the type of shipment and the goods to 
be transported. Validity is calculated from the date on which the EWB has been 
generated (relevant date) and the period of validity shall be counted from the date and 
time of EWB generation and shall extend for the number of days as prescribed based 
on the distance to be travelled. 
3.4.5 Extension of EWB 
The validity of the EWB can be extended by the Commissioner, on the 
recommendations of the GST Council4, by notification for certain categories of 
specified goods. Validity can also be extended under circumstances wherein the goods 
cannot be transported within the validity period within eight hours from the time of its 
expiry. 
3.4.6 Cancellation of EWB 
Where an EWB has been generated under the rule ibid, but goods are either not 
transported or are not transported as per the details furnished in the EWB, the same may 
be cancelled electronically on the common portal, within 24 hours of generation of the 
EWB. However, an EWB cannot be cancelled if it has been verified in -transit in 
accordance with the provisions of Rule 138B of the HPGST Rules, 2017. 
3.4.7 Acceptance/ Rejection of EWB 
The details of the EWB generated will be communicated electronically to the supplier 
and the recipient, and the consignee is required to communicate his acceptance or 
rejection of the goods being transported. If the consignee does not communicate his 
acceptance or rejection within seventy-two hours of the details being communicated to 
him or the time of delivery of goods, whichever is earlier, the EWB shall be deemed to 
be accepted. 
3.4.8 Blocking/Unblocking of EWB 
Blocking of EWB generation was implemented from 01  December 2019. Thus, 
taxpayers (either as Consignor, Consignee or Transporter) are disabled from generating 
EWB in the case of non-filing of monthly/quarterly returns for a consecutive period of 
two or more tax periods.  
Suspended taxpayer cannot generate EWB as a consignor/transporter . However, as a 
consignee/transporter, they can receive a generated EWB. Unblocking of EWB 
 
4  Section 2(36) of HPGST Act 2017 “Council” means the Goods and Services Tax Council 
established under article 279A of the Indian Constitution. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
52 | P a g e  
generation facility can be done either by the consignor or jurisdictional officer in the 
event of filing returns for the default periods. 
Audit Objectives, Criteria, Scope & Sampling Methodology  
The Performance Audit covers critical examination of the overall performance of EWB 
system in the GST regime with reference to the EWBs issued during the audit period 
as referred at Paragraph 3.7. The Audit objective s, criteria, scope, sampling 
methodology determined for conducting the Performance Audit on EWB under GST 
are discussed in the following paragraphs. The taxpayers’ compliance, scrutiny process 
and subsequent preventive action s taken by the department on EWBs raised by the 
consignor/consignee/transporter have been examined. 
3.5 Audit Objectives 
This Performance Audit (PA) was conducted with the following Audit Objectives: 
1. Whether EWB mechanism is effective in protecting revenue interest of the 
Government. 
2. Whether the Preventive/ Enforcement activities of the Department in enforcing 
EWB provisions are efficient and effective. 
3.6
 Audit Criteria 
The PA on EWB system under GST has been evaluated against the following audit 
criteria: 
» Sections 10, 17, 25, 29, 37, 39, 44, 68, 73, 74, 129, 130 etc. of Himachal Pradesh 
Goods and Services Tax Act, 2017 (HPGST Act). 
» Rules 59, 60, 61, 62, 80, 138, etc. of HPGST Rules, 2017.  
» Notifications / Circulars / Instructions authori sed by GST Council and issued 
by Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs (CBIC). 
» Advisories/Standard Operating Procedures issued by National Informatics  
Centre (NIC) and CBIC. 
3.7 Audit Scope 
EWB transactions of the persons registered in the EWB portal pertaining to the period 
between 1 April 2018 and 31 March 2022 were covered in the PA. Audit examined the 
overall performance of EWB system in the GST regime with reference to the Audit 
objectives. EWB data (generated) for the audit period has been extracted from Goods 
and Services Tax Network (GSTN) and has been analysed. Movement of conveyances 
by roadways alone has been considered for this audit and railway/airway/seaway EWBs 
have been excluded from the scope of this Audit. 
The scope of audit also involved evaluation of the preventive functions of the 
Department with reference to EWBs , viz., interception of vehicles, verification of 
documents, inspection of goods and action taken thereof. 

Chapter III: Performance Audit on E-Way Bill System 
53 | P a g e  
3.8 Audit Sampling Methodology 
A problem-centric approach has been attempted for this PA as EWB generation under 
GST is a necessary condition to precede any movement of goods , subject to the 
threshold limit . Samples for Audit  Objective-1 evolved based on  the Key Problem 
Areas (KPAs)/Risk Dimensions identified. The KPAs that constrain revenue realisation 
for the Government are provided in Appendix 3.1. 
For Audit Objective -1, a total of 127 EWB cases (120 cases as per Risk Dimensions 
identified and seven cases of cross PAN) pertaining to 52 taxpayers were selected for 
audit. 
Audit Objective -2 evaluat ed the problems associated with enforcement/  preventive 
activities viz., Operational Preparedness, Effectiveness of Anti -Evasion measures and 
Intra-department and Inter-department coordination. For Audit Objective-2, eight5 units 
out of 16 units6 (50 per cent of the units) were randomly selected using IDEA software. 
For sub -samples, the Audit called for the actual number of cases booked i.e., 
GST-EWB-03 (Part B) from the selected eight units. From the data received, 200 cases 
(25 booked cases from each selected unit) were randomly selected.  
Further, observations detected during the data analysis on the data extracted from 
GSTN (called Totality Observations) were issued at the Commissionerate level.  
An Entry Conference for this PA was held on 2 March 2023 with the Commissioner, 
State Taxes and Excise, Himachal Pradesh in which the audit objectives, sample 
selection, audit scope and audit methodology for the PA were discussed.  The Exit 
Conference was held on 29 November 2024 with Special Secretary, State Taxes & 
Excise to the Government of Himachal Pradesh. The replies received have been suitably 
incorporated in the report.

3.11 Substantive Audit Findings

The systemic and compliance issues noticed during the audit are discussed under each 
Key Risk Area to provide the impacts of the risks identified. 
3.11.1 Ineligible taxpayers continuing under the Composition Levy Scheme 
(CLS) 
In terms of Section 10 (1) of the HPGST Act, a registered person whose aggregate 
turnover in the preceding financial year did not exceed the threshold limit 8 may opt to 
pay tax under Composition Levy Scheme9. Section 10(2)(c) provides that he shall not 
be eligible to opt for the scheme, if engaged in making any inter-state outward supplies 
of goods and in terms of Section 10(3), this scheme shall lapse on the day his aggregate 
turnover during a financial year exceeds the limit specified.  
As per Section 61 of the HPGST Act, various returns filed by the taxpayers have to be 
scrutinised by the Proper Officer to verify the correctness of the returns, and suitable 
action has to be taken on any discrepancies or inconsistencies reflected in the returns. 
During the audit, four EWBs pertaining to three taxpayers  (Appendix 3.2), falling 
under three units 10 were test -checked. It was observed that two taxpayers were 
continuing under the Composition Scheme despite having undertaken inter-state 
outward supply of goods. While in one case, the taxpayer had generated two inter-state 
EWBs on the same invoice and paid taxes at the rate of one per cent under Composition 
Levy Scheme and later converted to Normal taxpayer (as illustrated below). These 
 
7  Detailed verification of transactions covered by EWBs and their respective returns by the taxpayer 
selected for audit.

taxpayers were required to be brought out of the scheme as they are liable to pay tax at 
normal rate with effect from the date of effecting inter-state supply at first instance.  
On this being pointed out (May -July 2023), the Department stated (September 2023) 
that the cases have been examined and intimation of tax ascertained under section 73(5) 
and notices under section 61 of the HPGST Act, 2017 have been issued. 
An illustrative case is given below: 
A taxpayer having GSTIN: 02 XXXXXXXXXX1Z7 registered and stayed  under 
Composition Scheme till 19 April 2019 and has been active as a normal taxpayer since 
20 April 2019. During the period 2018 -19, the taxpayer had generated  multiple inter-
state EWBs on the same invoice and charged tax at the rate of 18 per cent (nine per cent 
CGST and nine per cent SGST). Scrutiny of records on GST portal revealed that while 
filing GSTR-9A, the taxpayer had paid taxes at the rate of one per cent under 
Composition Levy Scheme.  
On this being pointed out (May 2023), the Department replied (May 2023) that notice 
under Section 70 has been issued to the taxpayer to provide the records in this regard.  
Recommendation 1:  Department should incorporate validation control in EWB 
system to alert the taxpayer as well as the departmental officer when a CLS taxpayer 
generates EWB for inter-state supply.  
3.11.2 Generation of EWBs by the taxpayers who had filed Nil Returns 
As per Section 61 of the HPGST Act, various returns filed by the taxpayers have to be 
scrutinised by the Proper Officer to verify the correctness of the returns, and suitable 
action has to be taken on any discrepancies or inconsistencies reflected in the returns.  
Audit test-checked 120 EWBs pertaining to 45 taxpayers and observed in the case of 
five taxpayers pertaining to four units11 (Appendix 3.3) that taxpayers had generated 
74 EWBs of assessable value of ₹103.94 lakh. Even though the EWBs were generated, 
the taxpayers filed nil GSTR-1 for the respective return periods and had not discharged 
the tax liability on the same. 
On this being pointed out (May-July 2023), the Department replied in one case that the 
taxpayer had voluntarily made due payment after being pointed out by Audit. While in 
other cases, either they had created demand  vide DRC -07 or notices in the form of 
DRC-01A have been issued to the taxpayers. 
An illustrative case is given below: 
Audit Scrutiny of the GSTIN (02XXXXXXXXXX1Z8) for the financial year 2019-20 
revealed that the taxpayer  had taken GST Registration on 10  November 2019 and 
generated EWBs during December 2019. Further, scrutiny of GST returns showed that 
the taxpayer was active till 02  January 2020 and had generated EWB but filed Nil 
 
11  DCST&E Kangra at Dharmshala, Sirmaur at Nahan, Solan and Una.

GSTR-1 and GSTR -3B Returns. The taxpayer had also not filed  Annual Return 
GSTR-9 and Final Return GSTR 10. 
Since the taxpayer had filed nil GSTR-1 and GSTR -3B returns (for November 2019 
and December 2019), it resulted in loss of revenue to the State exchequer to the tune of 
₹ 10.37 lakh (Appendix 3.4). 
On being pointed out by Audit, DCSTE, Solan while confirming the facts and figures 
replied (May 2023) that there was no record of any Show cause notice (SCN) issued to 
the taxpayer in this connection, a vailable on GST portal. No penalty for non -filer and 
nil filer of GSTR-9 and GSTR-10 had been imposed. It was also stated by the DCSTE, 
Solan that v erification of EW Bs might have skipped from the mind of the Proper 
Officer. Keeping in view the excessive workload on Proper Officers (ACST&Es), it is 
not possible to make 360-degree scrutiny of the taxpayers before cancelling the GSTIN. 
Since both the returns were supposed to be filed after the cancellation of GSTIN, it is 
impossible for Proper Officer to scrutinise returns of each taxpayer in his jurisdiction.  
Further, the department replied (April 2024) that demand for ₹31.07 lakh vide DRC 07 
has been created. 
While the constraints are appreciated, nonetheless, the Proper Officer should have taken 
a proactive approach to protect the Government revenue. Further, EWB portal should 
be integrated with GST portal so that details of outward EWBs generated should reflect 
automatically in GSTR-1 of the taxpayer. 
3.11.3 Generation of EWBs by Non-Filers of GST Returns 
In terms of Section 29 (2) of the HPGST Act, the Proper Officer  may cancel the 
registration of the taxpayer when a composition taxpayer has not furnished returns for 
three consecutive tax periods; or any other registered person has not furnished returns 
for a continuous period of six months. 
According to Section 62 of the Act ibid, where a registered person fails to furnish the 
returns even after the service of a notice, the Proper Officer may proceed to assess the 
tax liability of the said person to the best of his judgement , taking into account all the 
relevant material which is available or which he has gathered and has issued an 
assessment order.  In cases, where a taxable person whose registration has been 
cancelled but who was liable to pay tax, the Proper Officer may proceed to assess the  
tax liability of such taxable person to the best of his judgement as per provisions of 
Section 63 of the Act.  
As per Section 30(1) of the said Act, any registered person whose registration is 
cancelled, may apply for revocation of cancellation of the registration in the prescribed 
manner within thirty days from the date of service of the cancellation order. 
Rule 138E of HPGST Rules imposes restriction on persons including consignors, 
consignees, transporter or an e-commerce operator or a courier agency to generate 
EWBs in respect of a registered person who has not filed relevant GST Returns for 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
58 | P a g e  
prescribed consecutive periods 12. The blocking functionality has been enabled on the 
EWB Common Portal with effect from 01 December 2019.  
From the data analysis, Audit observed that 12  taxpayers (Appendix 3.5) generated 
3,844 EWBs  during the period 2018 -19 to 2021 -22 having assessable value of 
₹183.21 crore but did not file GSTR 3B returns in the corresponding months, hence no 
tax liability was discharged. Due to non-filing of returns, the Department cancelled the 
registration of these taxpayers under Section 29 of the HPGST Act. Further, it was 
observed that two taxpayers (Appendix 3.6) had taken new registrations13 on the same 
PAN instead of revocation of cancellation. However, no action was initiated to assess 
the tax liability as specified under Sections 62 and 63 of the HPGST Act. 
On this being pointed out (May -July 2023), the Department in case of nine taxpayers 
pertaining to six units14 (Appendix 3.7) replied (May -November 2023) that after 
scrutiny, notices in the form of DRC -01A and under section 61 of the act have been 
issued to the taxpayers. 
An illustrative case is given below: 
Audit scrutiny of GSTR -1, GSTR-3B, GSTR-2A and Comparison Sheet available on 
GST portal in respect of a taxpayer (GSTIN: 02 XXXXXXXXXX2ZG) under the 
jurisdiction of the DCST&E , BBN Baddi, revealed that the  taxpayer was a non -filer 
due to which the  Department had cancelled (05  March 2021) the registration of the 
taxpayer. Scrutiny further revealed that t he taxpayer had not filed GSTR -1 (for the 
months of January 2019 and March 2019), GSTR 3B (except April 2018) and had not 
discharged tax liability on outward supplies for the financial year 2018-19, to the tune 
of ₹16.65 crore (excluding interest/penalty , if any) (Appendix 3.8). The taxpayer , 
instead of applying for revocation of cancellation , had taken a new registration with 
effect from 01 June 2022 on the same PAN.  
Audit noted that Department had alrea dy initiated action vide DRC -01 against 
generation of EWB and outward supplies made but the taxpayer had not made payment 
of due tax under Section 76 of the Act ibid. However, the r eply of the Department 
regarding the observation and current status was awaited (March 2025). 
3.11.4 Generation of EWBs by cancelled taxpayers 
As per Section 63 of the HPGST Act, where a taxable person whose registration has 
been cancelled; but who was liable to pay tax, the Proper Officer may proceed to assess 
the tax liability of such taxable person to the best of his judgement. A cancelled taxpayer 
cannot generate EWBs, as the taxpayer may pass Input Tax Credit (ITC) without filing 
of returns, resulting in non-payment of tax.

During the audit, three taxpayers falling under three units15 were test-checked and it 
was noticed that the taxpayers had generated 15 EWBs for effecting outward supplies 
of ₹4.88 crore (Appendix 3.9) after the effective date of cancellation of their GST 
registration. Audit further observed that none of the cancelled taxpayers had filed 
GSTR-10 returns after cancellation of registrations and the Department had not initiated 
action to assess taxpayer’s pending tax liabilities. 
On this being pointed out (May -July 2023), the Department in case of the taxpayer 
(GSTIN: 02 XXXXXXXXXX1Z5) replied (May 2023) that the taxpayer had filed 
application (on 22 January 2018) for cancellation of registration and action was initiated 
for cancellation on 18 December 2019. The registered taxpayer was active at the time 
of EWB generation and his registration was cancelled thereafter on 18 December 2019 
i.e., the registration was cancelled retrospectively (effective from 22  February 2019). 
The reply of the Department is not acceptable as the  Department should have taken 
action as per Section 62 of th e Act to assess and recover the tax due especially as the 
registration was cancelled retrospectively. 
An illustrative case is given below: 
Audit scrutiny of records available on the GSTN portal in respect GSTIN: 
02XXXXXXXXXX1ZB under the jurisdiction of the DCST&E Una, indicated that the 
Department had suo -moto cancelled its registration on 16  February 2021. Scrutiny 
further revealed that the taxpayer had generated 11 EWBs hav ing an assessable value 
of ₹4.84 crore beyond the effective date of cancellation of GST Registration.  
On being pointed out by the Audit (July 2025) , the department replied ( September 
2023) that the JCSTE Una has created an additional demand (DRC01 -A) of 
₹146.50 lakh on the basis of wrongful claim of ITC purely on paper transactions 
without actual movement of goods against. The department had rejected the 
inadmissible ITC to the tune of ₹68.17 lakh for the period April 2021 to March 2022. 
While the department  has now taken action against the taxpayer, the reply of the 
Department does not address the fact that the proper officer should have assessed the 
taxpayer’s GST liability while cancelling the registration.  
Recommendation 2: Department should consider issuing suitable instructions to the 
proper officers for considering EWBs generated by the taxpayer before cancellation 
of registration retrospectively and taking action for recovery of tax wherever 
applicable. 
3.11.5 Generation of multiple EWBs on the strength of same/similar Invoices 
As per Rule 46 (b) of HPGST Rules, a tax invoice shall be issued by the registered 
person containing consecutive unique serial number, not exceeding sixteen characters, 
for a financial year. 
 
15  DCST&E BBN Baddi, Solan and Una. 
 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
60 | P a g e  
As per Para 5-1 of the User Manual issued by the NIC, the taxpayer while generating 
the EWB is required to enter the Document Number relating to the consignment. The 
Document Number entered in should be unique. Invoice Number is the Document 
Number in respect of consignments relating to supplies. Hence, only one EWB is 
required to be generated based on each invoice. 
Audit test-checked 13 taxpayers who had generated multiple EWBs with the support of 
single invoice. Audit scrutinised corresponding returns and observed that 11 taxpayers 
pertaining to five units 16 used same invoice to generate multiple EWBs for movement 
of goods. Though this resulted in multiple consignments, Audit could not confirm, due 
to non-production of GSTR-1 invoice details, whether the  taxpayers had reported any 
consignments or not , in their returns, which could have led to non/short discharge of 
tax liability to the extent of ₹29.71 lakh . Details on revenue impact (inclusive of all 
duplicate consignments) are shown in Appendix 3.10. 
On this being pointed out by Audit (May-July 2023), the Department , in case of nine 
taxpayers, replied (May -September 2023) that the scrutiny has been done and 
notice/summons under section 61/70/74(5) of the HPGST Act, 2017 have been issued. 
Further, in case of two taxpayers, response of the department was awaited 
(March 2025). 
An illustrative case is given below: 
Scrutiny in case of GSTIN: 02 XXXXXXXXXX1ZI revealed that the taxpayer had 
generated a total of 14 EWBs having an assessable value of ₹12.85 lakh using only six 
distinct17 invoice numbers during the financial year 2018 -19 as detailed in  
Appendix 3.11. This indicated lack of validation controls in the EWB Common Portal 
to restrict generation of multiple EWBs using same or similar Invoices. 
On this being pointed out by Audit (July 2023), the Department replied (September 
2023) that notice (ASMT 10) to the taxpayer has been issued under Section 61of the 
HPGST Act, 2017. 
Recommendation 3: Department should incorporate validation control in the EWB 
system to alert taxpayer and departmental officer on generation of multiple EWBs 
with same invoice/similar invoice. 
3.11.6 Non/short discharge of tax liability by taxpayers  
As per Section 37 of the HPGST Act, every registered person other than composition 
taxpayers shall furnish the details of outward supplies of goods or services or both 
effected during a tax period in GSTR-1 and to discharge tax liability thereon in GSTR-
3B. In respect of taxpayers who have opted for composition scheme, they shall pay tax 
in GSTR-4/GST-CMP-08.

in respect of three units20, i t was replied  (May-November 2023) that after scrutiny , 
notices under Sections 61 and 65 have been issued to the taxpayers. Reply from one 
unit (DCST&E, BBN Baddi) for the remaining three cases was awaited (March 2025).  
An illustrative case is given below: 
Audit scrutiny of GSTR -1, GSTR-3B, GSTR-2A and Comparison Sheet available on 
GST portal in respect of taxpayer GSTIN: 02 XXXXXXXXXX2ZX under the 
jurisdiction of the DCST&E Shimla, revealed that the taxpayer was active from 01 July 
2017. The Department cancelled its registration on 30  September 2019 on receipt of 
application from the taxpayer. Scrutiny on EWB portal further revealed that the 
taxpayer had generated various E WBs for outward supplies from April 2018 to 
September 2019 , on which tax liability of ₹59.17 lakh was required to be paid but 
taxpayer had filed GSTR-1 and GSTR-3B for the same period and had discharged tax 
liability of ₹39.15 lakh only. This resulted in revenue loss of ₹20.02 lakh (excluding 
interest/penalty, if any) to the Government exchequer. 
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied (October 2023) that the 
notice in ASMT-10 had been issued to the taxpayer in July 2023. Further, department 
replied (March 2024) that the case has been finalised up to the financial year 2019-20. 
However, the reply of the Department is silent about the reasons for not assessing the 
tax liability correctly at the time of cancellation of registration.

3.11.7 Excess Availing of Input Tax Credit (ITC) 
As per Section 16 of the HPGST Act, every registered person shall be entitled to take 
credit of input tax (ITC) charged on any supply of goods or services or both  to him 
which are used or intended to be used in the course or furtherance of his business  and 
the said amount shall be credited to the electronic credit ledger of such person. Further, 
in terms of Sub -section 2(c) of the said Section, the claim of ITC is subject to the 
condition, inter alia, that the tax charged in respect of such supply has been actually 
paid to the Government.  
Audit test-checked 120 EWBs of 45 taxpayers to ensure the correctness of availment 
of ITC. During scrutiny of returns, it was observed that nine taxpayers pertaining to 
four units21 had availed ITC of ₹335.26 crore against the inward supplies. However, 
Audit noticed from the GSTR-2A of the taxpayers that the ITC available for them was 
only ₹314.92 crore. Hence, the availment of ITC amounting to ₹20.34 crore by these 
taxpayers was not in order which resulted in loss of revenue to Government exchequer 
to the extent ITC availed in excess (Appendix 3.13). 
On this being pointed out (May -July 2023), the Department in respect of three units22 
replied (May-November 2023) that notices under Sections 61 and 65 of the Act have 
been issued to the taxpayers. Reply from one unit (DCST&E BBN Baddi) was awaited 
(March 2025).  
An Illustrative case is given below: 
Audit scrutiny of GSTR -1, GSTR-3B, GSTR-2A and Comparison Sheet available on 
GST portal in respect of GSTIN: 02XXXXXXXXXX1ZP for the period from 2018-19 
to 2021-22 under the jurisdiction of the DCST&E Solan revealed that the taxpayer had 
availed ITC of ₹1.67 crore in his GSTR -3B instead of the ITC available ₹1.62 crore 
during the period from 2019-20 to 2020-21 in his GSTR-2A. This resulted into excess 
availment of ITC of ₹5.55 lakh. 
On this being pointed out (May -July 2023), the Department replied (June 2023) that 
after scrutiny, notice (ASMT 10) under Section 61 of the HPGST Act 2017  has been 
issued to the taxpayer.  
3.11.8 Mismatch in GSTR 2A and Inward EWB 
As per Section 37 of the HPGST Act , every registered person, other than an Input 
Service Distributor, a non -resident taxable person and a person paying tax under the 
provisions of Section 10 or Section 51 or Section 52, shall furnish the details of outward 
supplies of goods or services or both effected during a tax period in GSTR -1. The 
details provided by the suppliers in GSTR-1 will be auto-populated to the recipients of 
the goods in GSTR-2A to enable them to take ITC. 
 
21  DCST&E BBN Baddi, Nurpur, Solan and Una. 
22  DCST&E Nurpur, Solan and Una. 

Chapter III: Performance Audit on E-Way Bill System 
63 | P a g e  
Returns of seven cases falling in four units23 were test-checked for cross PAN analysis. 
Audit scrutiny of REG -01, GSTR -1, GSTR -3B, GSTR -2A and Comparison Sheet 
available on GST portal in respect of taxpayer GSTIN: 02XXXXXXXXXX1ZL under 
the jurisdiction of DCST&E BBN B addi revealed that the taxpayer  (consignee) had 
done transactions with other GSTIN (consignor) issued on  the same PAN  
(03XXXXXXXXXX1ZJ) in other State  (Punjab). The reconciliation difference was 
noticed as the taxpayer received goods through an E WB no. 381 XXXXXX391 
(from GSTIN 03 XXXXXXXXXX1ZJ) on 31  May 2018 with document no . - 1X7- 
31 May 2018. The total invoice value of the EWB was ₹2,17,12,000 (including IGST: 
₹33,12,000), whereas in the GSTR 2A of the consignee for the month of May 2018, the 
invoice value was ₹21,71,200 (including IGST ₹3,31,200).  
The under-reporting of outward supplies by the consignor in his GSTR-1 also effected 
the inward supplies of the consignee in GSTR  2A. This may have led to tax evasion 
both by the consignor and the consignee. The Department should have informed the 
respective inter-state jurisdictional tax authorities about the reconciliation difference to 
protect the Government revenue. 
The discrepancy was reported (June 2023) to the Department and the reply was awaited 
(March 2025). During the Exit Conference (November 2024), the Department stated  
the generation of EWBs from the GST portal is not conclusive evidence  that the 
transaction/sale of goods has actually taken place.  However, the reply of the 
Department is not explaining the reason for the difference between the amount 
reported/reflected in EWB and the GSTR1 and GSTR 2A of the consignor and the 
consignee respectively.

Preventive function of the Department 
The preventive function in the GST department holds paramount importance in 
maintaining the integrity of the tax system. In essence, the preventive function is a 
cornerstone in establishing a fair, transparent, and effective GST framework, benefiting 
both the government and taxpayers alike. The Performance Audit on EWB system 
under GST for years 2018 -19 to 2021 -22 was conducted on overall performance of 
EWB system in the GST regime with reference to the EWBs issued during the Audit 
period i.e., 2018-2022. In this chapter, the preventive function in the GST department 
in respect of EWB system (Audit Objective 2) has been examined by Audit. 
3.13 Preventive measures to enforce EWB provisions 
Audit studied EWB related functions of the  units of the State with specific focus on 
(i) Operational preparedness, (ii) Effectiveness of anti-evasion measures and 
(iii) Intra-departmental coordination in monitoring EWB related transactions. For this 
purpose, Audit selected eight units25 as sample falling under Commissionerate of State 
Taxes and Excise, Himachal Pradesh Shimla. During verification, Audit noticed certain 
deficiencies and shortcomings in the activities undertaken by the units, which are 
detailed in the following paragraphs. 
 
25  Jt. Commissioner, ST&E (SEZ Parwanoo, CEZ Una), DCST&Es (BBN Baddi, Bilaspur, 
Hamirpur, Kangra at Dharamshala, Shimla and Solan). 

Chapter III: Performance Audit on E-Way Bill System 
65 | P a g e  
3.14  Operational Preparedness of the Department 
 
3.14.1 Dedicated Setup/unit 
A dedicated unit for EWB related enforcement activities like verification of EWBs 
during interception of vehicles and follow up action, wherever required, utilising EWB 
Analytical Reports in planning the EWB verification, etc. will improve the efficiency 
of preventive functions.  
However, Audit noticed that out of eight units, six units26 had not formed any dedicated 
setup for EWB related enforcement activities. These units had only formed Inspection 
teams, whenever required, with the existing staff. Two units (DCST&E, BBN Baddi 
and Shimla) did not provide information. 
It is crucial for the Department to prioritise and invest in dedicated resources for EWB 
verification to ensure the smooth functioning of the GST system and maintain the 
integrity of the tax framework. 
3.14.2 Adequacy of manpower 
Out of eight units taken up for audit, two units (JCST&E CEZ Una and DCST&E 
Hamirpur) provided the details of manpower in existence. It was observed from the 
details provided by the JCST&E CEZ, Una that there was overall no vacancy in the 
Unit. Further DCST&E, Hamirpur provided only men -in-position of ACST&E, State 
Taxes and Excise Officer ( STEO) and Assistant State Taxes and Excise Officer 
(ASTEO) and not the sanctioned strength. Six units27 had not provided information. 
Due to non -availability of dedicated setup in the unit for EWB verification, the 
percentage of vacancy could not be determined. Non -availability of dedicated set -up 
will have a direct impact on EWB related preventive functions as can be seen from the 
reply of one unit28. The unit had stressed that Proper Officers are on excessive workload 
and verification of EWBs might skip from the mind of the Proper Officer. It is also not 
possible to make 360 degree scrutiny of each taxpayer in his jurisdiction. 
Hence, it is essential for the Department to ensure that an adequate number of trained 
personnel are deployed for EWB verification. 
3.14.3 Lack of sufficient patrol vehicles 
Audit noticed that out of eight units, two units (JCST&E SEZ Parwanoo and DCST&E 
Bilaspur) had neither requested for patrolling vehicle nor  allocated the vehicles 
exclusively for EWB verifications. In these units, the verifications were generally 
conducted using the vehicles hired for other office purposes.

It was further noticed that only four units29 have made specific requisitions for patrol 
vehicles ranging from one to nine for the purpose of EWB verifications against which 
they had received the requisitioned vehicles.  
The absence of patrol vehicles for EWB verification in the Department may have 
impacted operational efficiency as the units could not achieve set targets of EWB 
verification as explained in Paragraph 3.14.4. Not having patrol vehicles resulted in 
limited mobility and making on -site verifications, challenging. Hence the Department 
should have a balanced and well-maintained fleet tailored for EWB verification, which 
can contribute to the efficient enforcement of EWB regulations and overall GST 
compliance. 
On this being pointed out (September 2023), the Government stated  in the Exit 
Conference (November 2024) that manpower/patrol vehicles will be provided for 
dedicated setups in the Department. 
3.14.4 Targets and achievements 
The Commissioner of State Taxes and Excise, Himachal Pradesh in his letter dated 
6 October 2018 communicated the State’s decision to improve enforcement activities 
in order to bridge the GST revenue gap by improving EWB implementation in the State. 
To achieve this, it was decided to hire additional vehicles at field level to improve GST 
enforcement. As such, it was envisaged to ensure generation of three times revenue of 
cost of hiring of checking vehicles. Further, in August 2021, to put focused efforts in  
GST collection for revival of economy after Covid-19 pandemic, the Department 
decided to focus on EWB verification and fixed targets for each revenue district in 
respect of EWB verification. 
Audit called for details of targets fixed by the Commissionerate of State Taxes and 
Excise Himachal Pradesh, Shimla for verification of EWBs through vehicle 
interceptions and achievements against them from eight units. Replies received from 
seven units30 revealed that these units did not have specific targets for conducting 
verification of EWBs during the 2018-19 to 2021-22 as detailed in Table 3.4.

3.14.5 Usage of MIS Reports/ Usage of Analytical Reports of other Agencies 
NIC generates Analytical Reports on EWB transactions and shares the same with the 
GST Departments under Centre and State units. Audit studied the extent to which these 
Reports were utilised by the Units for planning the verifications of EWBs. The results 
are summarised below: 
(i) It was noticed that in six units31, the access to comprehensive analytical roles of 
EWB were with the Joint Commissioners/ Deputy Commissioners and few analytical 
reports were viewable to Assistant Commissioners of units. Two Units (DCST&E BBN 
Baddi and Shimla) had not provided information in this regard. 
(ii) Five units32 stated that they were using the Analytical Reports for planning the 
vehicles interception. However, none of these five units have provided the supporting 
documents for audit scrutiny. Hence , Audit could not ascertain the effectiveness of 
usage of these Reports.  Regarding other rationales behind planning the vehicle 
interception program, none of the eight units provided details of methodology adopted 
for selection. Three units33 had not provided information on usage of the Analytical 
reports. 
(iii) In eight units, it was noticed that the Analytical Reports were not shared with the 
jurisdictional officers for using them in scrutiny of Returns. 
(iv) It was observed that none of the eight units forwarded any requirement for new 
MIS reports to NIC. 
(v) None of the eight units were receiving any analytical reports from Directorate 
General of Analytics & Risk Management (DGARM) or any other agencies based on 
EWB. The details on frequency and action taken on the reports, though called for, were 
still awaited from these units. 
 
31  JCST&E CEZ Una , JCST&E SEZ Parwanoo , DCST&Es Bilaspur, Hamirpur, Kangra at 
Dharmshala and Solan. 
32  JCST&E CEZ Una, JCST&E SEZ Parwanoo, DCST&Es Bilaspur, Hamirpur and Solan. 
33  DCST&Es BBN Baddi, Kangra at Dharamshala and Shimla. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
68 | P a g e  
Using Analytical Reports or Management Information System (MIS) reports 
effectively for the EWBs by Department could avoid repercussions on both operational 
efficiency and compliance. These reports serve as a vital tool for the Department to 
monitor and regulate the movement of goods, ensuring adherence to tax regulations and 
facilitating effective enforcement. Without the insights provided by analytical reports, 
the Department may not detect patterns of tax evasion, identify potential fraud, or 
efficiently allocate resources for enforcement activiti es. It could also impede the 
department’s ability to analyse trends, assess taxpayer compliance, and make informed 
policy decisions. Additionally, without real -time visibility into EWB data, the 
Department may face challenges in responding promptly to issues such as tax leakages. 
In the long run,  using analytical and MIS tools would strengthen the Department’s 
effectiveness in enforcing tax laws, compromising revenue collection, and eroding the 
overall integrity of the tax system. 
Recommendation 4: The analytical reports generated by National Informatics Centre 
(NIC) on risky e-Way Bill transactions may be utilised by Departmental officers for 
identifying probable cases of tax evasion.

procedures as per the State circulars but sufficient opportunity of being heard was given

implementation. The Department should have adhered in spirit to the notifications, 
circulars and procedures prescribed for EWB verification, detention, confiscation and 
release of goods and vehicle in movement. 
3.15.2 Not raising demand for tax and penalty collected during EWB verification 
According to Section 129 (1) of the HPGST Act, where any person transports any goods 
in contravention of the provisions of this Act or the rules made thereunder, all such 
goods and conveyance used as a means of transport for carrying the said goods and 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
70 | P a g e  
documents relating to such goods and conveyance shall be liable to detention or seizure 
and after detention or seizure, shall be released, (a)  on payment of the applicable tax 
and penalty equal to one hundred per cent of the tax payable on such goods35 and, in 
case of exempted goods, on payment of an amount equal to two per cent of the value 
of goods or twenty -five thousand rupees, whichever is less, where the owner of the 
goods comes forward for payment of such tax and penalty. 
According to the Guidelines issued by the State tax department 36, the order in Form 
GST MOV-09 shall be uploaded on the common portal. The demand accruing from the 
proceedings shall be added in the Electronic Liability Register and, upon payment of 
the demand, such register shall be credited by either debiting the electronic cash ledger 
or the electronic credit ledger of the concerned person in accordance with the provisions 
of Section 49 of the CGST Act.  
Audit noticed in nine booked cases of four units37 that neither was the tax liability in 
Electronic Liability register created by the Department by issuing DRC-07 nor was the 
tax liability adjusted by the taxpayer voluntarily in his Electronic Liability register by 
DRC-03. The payment in Electronic Cash ledger was also not debited to set -off the 
liability under electronic liability register. This resulted in to loss of Government 
revenue to the extent of ₹3.06 lakh. 
On this being pointed out by Audit (August-October 2023), the Department stated that 
it has taken preventive action in five cases recovering ₹1.68 lakh (Appendix 3.15) and 
liability has been set-off. Department further stated that the cases were detected in the 
very beginning of implementation of EWB system and due to technical issues in the 
network, the liabilities were not created in the electronic liability register of the 
taxpayers. The necessary procedure to create the demand in Electronic Liability register 
of the taxpayers is being initiated and notice is being issued to recover the due tax and 
penalties. 
Audit acknowledges the action taken by the Department in creating the demand in 
electronic liability register as m erely depositing money into cash ledger does not set -
off liability. The amount available in the electronic cash ledger may be used for making 
any other payments towards tax, interest, penalty, fees or any other amount payable 
under the provisions of the Act or the rules made thereunder, thus creating a demand in 
electronic liability register of the taxpayer is necessary.  
3.16 Intra-departmental Coordination in monitoring EWB related transactions 
As per section 129 of the HPGST Act , deals with the detention and seizure of goods 
where such goods are transported in contravention of the provisions of this Act or the 
rules made thereunder, all such goods and conveyance used as a means of transport for 
carrying the said goods and documents relating to such goods and conveyance shall be 
 
35 With effect from 01/01/2022, the amount payable was modified as penalty equal to two 
hundred per cent of the tax payable.  
36  Para 2(h) of Circular No.  41/15/2018-GST dated 13/04/2018  circulated by the State tax 
department. 
37  JCST&E SEZ Parwanoo, DCST&E BBN Baddi, Kangra at Dharamshala and Solan. 

Chapter III: Performance Audit on E-Way Bill System 
71 | P a g e  
liable to detention or seizure and such goods shall be released on payment of prescribed 
tax and penalty. Further, as per Section 17(5) of the Act “ITC shall not be available on 
any tax paid under the provisions of Section 129”.  
However, it was noticed that in four booked cases pertaining to DCST&E BBN Baddi 
having tax implication of ₹48,692 (Appendix 3.16), no information has been passed to 
concerned jurisdictional/non-jurisdictional authorities to block credits of the same. 
On this being pointed out (August -October 2023), the Department replied (November 
2023) that in one case no correspondence was made with the concerned jurisdictional 
authorities. Further , response of the Department for the remaining three cases was  
awaited (March 2025). 
Absence of any mechanism to send an alert message to the recipients and the Proper 
Officer concerned may result into availment of ITC on any taxes paid under 
Section 129. 
3.17  Levy of Penalty under HP GST Act, 2017 
Chapter XIX of HPGST Act 2017 provides Sections from 122-138 for various offences 
and penalties. Further, as per Rule 138(1) of HPGST Rules, 2017, every registered 
person who causes movement of goods of consignment value exceeding the prescribed 
limit, in relation to (i) supply; or (ii) for reasons other than supply; or (iii) due to inward 
supply from an unregistered person, shall, before commencement of such movement, 
furnish information relating to the said goods as specified in Part A of Form GST 
EWB-01, electronically, on the common portal along with such other information as 
may be required on the common portal and a unique number will be generated on the 
said portal.  
Audit scrutinised the records to ascertain the extent of adherence to the HPGST 
Act/Rules 2017 by the field units. For this, 200 booked cases pertaining to eight units 
were selected for scrutiny and the deviations noticed are discussed below: 
(a) According to Section 122(1) of HPGST Act 2017, where a taxable person who -
(i) supplies any goods or services or both without issue of any invoice or issues an 
incorrect or false invoice with regard to any such supply; he shall be liable to pay a 
penalty of ten thousand rupees or an amount equivalent to the tax evaded or the tax 
not deducted under section 51 or short deducted or deducted but not paid to the 
Government or tax not collected under Section 52 or short collected or collected but 
not paid to the Government or input tax credit availed of or passed on or distributed 
irregularly, or the refund claimed fraudulently, whichever is higher. 
Audit noticed that in two booked cases pertaining to two units (DCST&E Shimla 
and JCST&E SEZ Parwanoo), the Proper Officer had imposed penalty of ₹15,000 
instead of ₹25,530 while finalising the intercepted  cases. This resulted in loss of 
₹10,530 to the Government exchequer (Appendix 3.17). 
On this being pointed out (August -October 2023), the Department replied 
(September-November 2023) that the Proper Officer had imposed the penalty under 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
72 | P a g e  
Section 122 of the HPGST Act, 2017 equivalent to the tax evaded on the invoice 
produced by the person in charge of the goods. 
However, the penalty levied was lower of the two amounts, while the section 
requires levying of penalty at the higher of the prescribed penalty of ₹10,000 or the 
amount of tax leviable.  
(b) Further according to Section 125 of the HPGST Act, 2017, wherein any person who 
contravenes any of the provisions of this Act or any rules made thereunder for which 
no penalty is separately provided for in this Act, shall be liable to a general penalty 
which may extend to twenty five thousand rupees. 
Audit noticed that in seven booked cases pertaining to two units (DCST&E BBN 
Baddi and JCST&E SEZ Parwanoo) (Appendix 3.18), the Proper Officer  had 
finalised cases related to expired EWB under Section 125 of the Act whereas the 
cases should have been finali sed under section 129(a) of the Act. This resulted in 
short realisation of government revenue amounting to ₹3.37 lakh (Tax of ₹2.04 lakh 
and penalty of ₹1.33 lakh). 
On this being pointed out (August -October 2023), the Department replied 
(September-November 2023) that the Proper Officer at his best conscience under 
merits of each case had decided and imposed general penalty under Section 125 of 
the HPGST Act, 2017. 
(c) Audit further noticed that in five booked cases pertaining to three units 
(Appendix 3.19), the Proper Officer  had finali sed cases of non -availability of 
documents, invoice or EWB with the person in-charge of the vehicle under Section 
129 of the Act without ascertaining the proper assessment of tax. The Proper Officer 
should have invoked 130(2) of the Act for imposing tax, penalty and fine for 
offenses. The Proper Officer had not invoked the appropriate Section of the Act, 
which resulted in short realisation of Government revenue amounting to ₹1.59 lakh 
(Fine ₹1.54 lakh and Penalty ₹5,000). 
It was also noticed that the detained vehicles were released without imposing fine. 
Not invoking appropriate section of the Act might have led  to potential loss of 
Government revenue. 
On this being pointed out (August -October 2023), the Department did not accept 
the audit observations and replied ( September-November 2023) that under Section 
25 of the HPGST Act 2017 , read with Section 20 of the IGST Act 2017, the 
taxpayers have 30 days’ time to get registered under the GST Act from the date they 
become liable to be registered. However, since taxpayers were not registered 
taxpayers, they could not issue any invoice as contained in Section 31 of the Act. 
The Department further stated that the Section 130(2) could not be invoked in these 
cases as the amount of tax involved is too meagre or akin to five thousand rupees, 
for which even the spirit of the Act has specifically mandated in Section 126, which 
provides that a breach shall be considered as a minor breach if the amount of the 
tax involved is less than five thousand rupees. The Proper Officer  at his best 

Chapter III: Performance Audit on E-Way Bill System 
73 | P a g e  
conscience under merits of each case had decided and imposed general penalty 
under Section 125 of the HPGST Act, 2017. 
Audit acknowledges the Department’s reply that the Proper Officer at his best 
conscience under merits of each case had decided and imposed penalties and harsh 
penalties u/s 129 & 130 of HPGST Act are not justified on mere expiration of the time 
limit of EWB and the same cannot be applied unless the intent to prove tax evasion is 
established. The Audit, however, is of the view that t he Department may consider 
laying down a more objective criteria for imposing penalties so that the Proper Officer 
may determine the Sections to impose penalties in a more objective manner. 
3.18 Conclusion 
The Himachal Pradesh State Taxes & Excise Department has sixteen units in the State 
to examine the taxpayer’s compliance, scrutiny process and subsequent preventive 
action taken on EWB raised by the consignor/consignee/transporter. A sample of 127 
EWBs of 52 taxpayers in nine units38 for assessing the effectiveness of EWB 
mechanism in protecting revenue interest of the Government and 200 cases for 
enforcement/preventive activities of the Department for the period from April 2018 to 
March 2022 were selected for audit. 
During the Performance Audit of EWB System under GST for the State of Himachal 
Pradesh, Audit observed certain deficiencies in the EWB system.  
It was observed that the EWB system could not distinguish between inter-state and 
intra-state movement of goods by composition taxpayers and permitted generation of 
EWBs in both cases. There were instances where EWBs were generated but taxpayers 
filed nil GSTR-1 for the respective return periods and did not discharge his tax liability. 
It was further noticed that EWBs were generated by non -filer taxpayers whose 
registrations were cancelled without settling the tax liability.  
Audit observed that EWB portal permitted generation of multiple EWB on same 
invoice. In many cases, it was observed that although the taxpayer had filed GST returns 
regularly but had made short/non-payment of tax liabilities.  
During the evaluation of the enforcement/preventive activities viz., operational 
preparedness, effectiveness of anti-evasion measures and intra-department and 
inter-department coordination, it was observed that the Department had not established 
a dedicated formation for EWB verification which  may have improved the efficiency 
of preventive functions.  
It was further observed that the absence of patrol vehicles for EWB verification in the 
Department may have impacted operational efficiency and resulted in limited mobility 
and making on-site verifications challenging. In case of some units, the Department did 
not have specific targets for conducting verification of EWBs. 
 
38  DCST&E (BBN Baddi, Kangra at Dharmshala, Kullu, Mandi, RD Nurpur, Shimla, Sirmaur at 
Nahan, Solan and Una). 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
74 | P a g e  
Preventive units were not using the analytical reports designed by NIC for planning the 
vehicle’s interception. These Reports were not shared with the jurisdictional officers 
for using them in scrutiny of returns.  
3.19 Recommendations 
The Government should 
R-9. Incorporate validation control in EWB system to alert the taxpayer as well as 
the departmental officer when a taxpayer under Composition Levy Scheme 
generates EWB for inter-state supply. 
R-10. Consider issuing suitable instructions to the proper officers for considering 
EWBs generated by the taxpayer before cancellation of registration 
retrospectively and taking action for recovery of tax wherever applicable. 
R-11. Incorporate validation control in the EWB system to alert taxpayer and 
departmental officer on generation of multiple EWBs with s ame 
invoice/similar invoice. 
R-12. Ensure that the analytical reports generated by National Informatics Centre  
(NIC) on risky EWB transactions may be utilised by Departmental officers 
for identifying probable cases of tax evasion.

Chapter IV - Department’s Oversight on GST Payments and 
Returns Filing for the Year 2018-19 to 2020-21 
 
4.1 Introduction 
Introduction of Goods and Service Tax (GST) has replaced multiple taxes levied and 
collected by the Centre and States. GST, which came into effect from 01 July 2017, is 
a destination-based consumption tax on the supply of goods or services or both, levied 
on every value addition. The Centre and States simultaneously levy GST on a common 
tax base. Central GST (CGST) and State GST (SGST) /Union Territory GST (UTGST) 
are levied on intra -state supplies, and Integrated GST (IGST) is levied on inter -state 
supplies. 
Section 59 of the Himachal Pradesh GST (HPGST) Act, 2017  stipulates GST as a 
self-assessment based tax, whereby the responsibility for calculating tax liability, 
discharging the computed tax liability and filing returns is vested on the taxpayer. The 
GST returns must be filed online regularly on the common GST portal, failing which 
penalties will be payable. Even if the business has had no tax liability during a particular 
tax period, it must file a nil return mandatorily. Further, Section 61 of the Act, read with 
Rule 99 of HPGST Rules, stipulate that the Proper Officer may scrutinise the return and 
related particulars furnished by taxpayers, communicate discrepancies to the taxpayers 
and seek an explanation. 
This Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) was taken up considering the 
significance of the control mechanism envisaged for tax compliance and the oversight 
mechanism of the Excise and Taxation Department  of the Government of Himachal 
Pradesh in this new tax regime. 
4.2 Audit Objectives  
This audit was oriented towards providing assurance on the adequacy and effectiveness 
of systems and procedures adopted by the Department with respect to tax compliance 
under GST regime. Audit of ‘Department’s oversight on GST Payments and Return s 
filing’ was taken up with the following audit objectives to seek an assurance on  
whether: 
i. the rules and procedures were designed to secure an effective check on tax 
compliance and were being duly observed by taxpayers; and 
ii. the scrutiny procedures, internal audit and other compliance functions of the 
Circles were adequate and effective. 
4.3 Audit Methodology and Scope 
This SSCA was predominantly conducted based on data analysis, which highlighted 
risk areas and red flags pertaining to the period April 2018 to March 2021 and audit of 
the functions of selected Circles covering the period from April 2020 to March 2021. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
76 | P a g e  
Through data analysis , a set of 16 deviations1 were identified across the domains of 
Input Tax Credit (ITC), Discharge of tax liability, Registration and Return filing. Such 
deviations were followed up through a centralised audit (limited audit2), whereby these 
deviations were communicated to the relevant State departmental field formations3 and 
action taken by the jurisdictional formations on the identified deviations was 
ascertained without involving field visits. 
The limited audit  was supplemented by a detailed audit  involving field visits for 
verification of records available with the jurisdictional field formations. Returns and 
related attachments and information were accessed through the back-end system of the 
State Taxes and Excise Department application as much as feasible , to examine 
data/documents relating to taxpayers (viz., registration, tax payment, returns and other 
departmental functions).  
The detailed audit also involved accessing relevant granular records from the taxpayers 
such as invoices through the respective field formations . This apart, compliance 
functions of the departmental formation such as scrutiny of returns, were also reviewed 
in selected Circles. 
The audit of the functions of selected Circles covered the period April 2020  to 
March 2021. The SSCA covered only the State administered taxpayers. The field audit 
was conducted from November 2023 to February 2024. 
The Entry Conference of this SSCA was held on 6 July 2023 with the Commissioner, 
State Taxes and Excise  (ST&E), Himachal Pradesh  in which the audit objectives, 
sample selection, audit scope and methodology were discussed. The Exit Conference 
was held on 7 th May 2025 with the Special Secretary (State Taxes and Excise), 
Himachal Pradesh in which the audit findings were discussed. The detailed replies  
furnished by the Department in June 2025 have been suitably incorporated in the 
relevant paragraphs.

A data-driven approach was adopted for planning , also to determine the nature and 
extent of substantive audit.  The sample for this SSCA comprised a set of deviations 
identified through data analysis for limited audit that did not involve field visits; a 
sample of taxpayers for detailed audit that involved field visits and scrutiny of

Irregular ITC reversal. The  50 taxpayers selected for Detailed Audit comprised of 
Large5, Medium6 and Small7 strata taxpayers as well as taxpayers selected randomly.

4.6 Audit findings  
The audit findings are categorised into the following three categories: 
a. Audit of Circles 
b. Limited Audit  
c. Detailed Audit 
4.6.1 Audit of Circles 
A return is a statement of specified particulars relating to the business activity 
undertaken by taxpayers during a prescribed period. Every taxpayer is legally obligated 
to furnish a complete and correct return duly declaring the tax liability for a given period 
and taxes paid within the stipulated time. In a self-assessment regime, the significance 
of monitoring return filing by taxpayers acquires greater significance as the returns are 
the first mode of information about taxpayers and their respective business activities. 
4.6.1.1 Lack of action on late-filers and non-filers 
Section 46 of the HPGST Act 2017, read with Rule 68 of HPGST Rules 2017, stipulates 
issue of a notice in Form GSTR-3A requiring filing of return within fifteen days if the 
taxpayer had failed to file the return within the due date. In case the ta xpayer fails to 
file the return even after such notice, the Proper Officers may proceed to assess the tax 
liability of the said person to the best of their judgment, taking into account all the 
relevant material which is available or gathered and issue an assessment order in Form 
ASMT-13.  
Filing of returns is related to payment of tax as the due date for both the actions are the 
same, which implies risk of non-payment of tax/penalty in the case of non-filers. 

Chapter IV: Department’s Oversight on GST Payments and Returns Filing for the Year 2018-19 to 2020-21 
 
79 | P a g e  
Action initiated on non-filers but not completed (GSTR-3A issued): During Circle 
audit, it was observed that GSTR-3A notices were generated by system in 1,998 cases 
across 10 Circles during financial year 2020 -218 (November 2020 to March 2021 ). 
However, due to  non-maintenance of records in Circles, we could not ascertain  the 
actions initiated against those who had not complied  even after receiving such notices 
(Appendix-4.1). 
On this being pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024), the Department forwarded 
(June 2025) the replies of five Circles9 wherein it was stated  that action has been 
initiated in 109  cases10, a demand of ₹ 0.71 lakh has been created  in one case 11, 
registrations have been cancelled suo moto in 249 cases12, returns have been filed in 
four cases 13, action is yet to be initiated in 105 cases 14 and 66 cases have become time 
barred.  
The Cart Road C ircle stated that entire working under HPGST Act 2017 is online , 
hence, entire record is available online and regular notices are being issued on monthly 
basis to both non-filers and late filers. However, Audit found no records on the follow 
up action against non filers and late filers of returns after GSTR 3A was issued in the 
MIS reports.  
Recommendation 1:  
Reinforce Follow -Up on Non -Filers and Late -Filers: Establish a monitoring 
mechanism for prompt follow up of notices issued for non -filing and late-filing of 
GST returns and maintaining proper records of compliance.  
4.6.1.2 Not initiating scrutiny of returns 
As per Section 61 of the HPGST Act, various returns filed by taxpayers have to be 
scrutinised by the Proper Officer to verify the correctness of the returns, and suitable 
action has to be taken on any discrepancies or inconsistencies reflected in the returns. 
The Proper Officer15 designated for this purpose is the Circle Officer16. Further, Rule 99 
of the HPGST Rules, 2017 mandates that in case of discrepancies, if any, notice shall 
be communicated to the taxpayer to seek his explanation. 
Department issued detailed guidelines/SOP on scrutiny of returns with a view to ensure 
uniformity and to standardise the procedure for the scrutiny proceeding as pe r Section 
61 of the Act  on 8  May 2023 for financial year 2017 -18 and 2018-19. Audit of

functioning of 10 Circles for the period 20 20-21 revealed that Department did not 
conduct any scrutiny under Section 61 during the pe riod 2020-21 as per MIS report

During Exit conference (May 2025) , the Special Secretary (S tate Taxes & Excise)
