The reply is, however, silent on the fact that MIS reports show no records for scrutiny 
cases from 2017 onwards.  
4.6.1.3 Delay in initiating audit by tax authorities 
As per the Section 65 of the HPGST Act, 2017 , the Commissioner or any officer 
authorised by him, by way of a general or a specific order, may undertake audit of any 
registered person for such period, at such frequency and in such manner as may be 
prescribed. Section 2 (13) of the HPGST Act, 2017, defines “Audit” as the examination 
of records, returns and other documents maintained or furnished by the registered 
person under this Act or the rules made thereunder or under any other law for the time 
being in force to verify the correctness of turnover declared, taxes paid, refund claimed 
and input tax credit availed, and to assess his compliance with the provisions of this 
Act or the rules made there under. 
Audit observed (November 2023-January 2024) across 10 Circles that the Department 
had not conducted any audit under Section 65  of HPGST Act in the State  during 
2020-21.  
During Exit Conference (May 2025), the Special Secretary (ST&E) stated that scrutiny 
of taxpayers could not be carried out due to COVID -19 pandemic . The reply is, 
however, silent on the fact that MIS reports show no records for audit cases from 2017 
onwards.  
Recommendation 2:  
Institutionalise timely scrutiny and Operationalise Internal Audit Function: Institute 
a mechanism for timely scrutiny of returns under Section 61 and implement internal 
audit of registered taxpayers under Section 65 of the Act as a regular, risk -based 
function. Develop and implement annual audit plans, maintain audit trails, and 
monitor follow-up actions on audit findings to ensure accountability.  
4.6.1.4 Inadequate efforts to recover dues  
Section 78 stipulates that any amount payable by a taxable person in pursuance of an 
order passed under this Act, shall be paid by such person within a period of three months 
from the date of service of such order , failing which recovery proceedings shall be 
initiated. The time period can be less than three months in some special circumstances, 
if it is expedient in the interest of the Government revenue.  
Audit observed that in 256 cases across 10 Circles, recovery of ₹49.56  crore was 
pending (January 2024) from the taxpayers after issuing DRC -07 in 2020-21. There 
was nothing on record to show that the D epartment has made efforts to recover the 
Government revenue as envisaged in Section 79 of HPGST Act.  

Chapter IV: Department’s Oversight on GST Payments and Returns Filing for the Year 2018-19 to 2020-21 
 
81 | P a g e  
On this being pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024), the Department forwarded 
(June 2025) the replies of five Circles17 wherein it was stated that in 75 cases, a recovery 
of ₹2.09 crore have been made; in three cases a recovery of ₹0.96 crore has been waived 
off; and  in two cases  registrations have been cancelled and recovery process is 
underway.  
However, recovery of ₹45.22 crore in 181 cases18 is still pending (Appendix-4.2).  
Recommendation 3:  
Strengthen Recovery Proceedings: Develop a mechanism for tracking cases where 
DRC-07 has been issued. Mandate periodic reporting from field officers on recovery 
progress and enforce timely initiation of recovery proceedings under Section 79 in 
cases of non-compliance.  
4.6.1.5 Cancellation of registrations 
Section 29 of the HPGST Act 2017 read with Rule 20 of the HPGST Rules allows for 
cancellation of registration by the taxpayer in certain situations like closure of business, 
turnover falling below threshold for registration, transfer of business/merger/  
amalgamation, change of PAN, non -commencement of business within the stipulated 
time period and death of the proprietor. The taxpayer applying for cancellation of 
registration should apply in REG-16 on the GST common portal within a period of 30 
days of the “occurrence of the event warranting the cancellation”.  
Section 29(2) of the HPGST Act allows for suo moto cancellation of the registration of 
taxpayer by tax officer on the grounds of contravention of the Acts or Rules by the 
taxpayer, composition  taxpayers not filing return for three consecutive tax periods, 
normal taxpayers not filing return for continuous period of six months, registered 
persons not commencing business within six months from date of registration and 
registration obtained by means of fraud, willful misstatement or suppression of facts. 
Section 45 of the HPGST Act requires every registered person other than (a) ISD or a 
non-resident taxable person or (b) Composition taxable person (Section 10) or 
(c) persons paying tax under Section 5 2 - Tax collection at source (TCS) or persons 
paying tax under Section 5 1 - Tax deducted at source (TDS), whose registration has 
been cancelled, to file a final return in GSTR -10, within three months of the effective 
date of cancellation or the date of order of cancellation, whichever is later. The purpose 
of the final return is to ensure that the taxpayer discharges the outstanding liability. In 
case of non-filing of GSTR -10, the same procedure as adopted for non -filing of any 
return must be followed by the tax officer. 
Audit observed various deficiencies in cancellation of registrations, which are brought 
out below:  
 
17  Baddi-I; Barotiwala; Cart Road; Mandi-I; Nalagarh-II.  
18  Of the ₹49.56 crore in 256 cases, ₹4.34 crore relating to 75 cases was deducted following the 
Department’s scrutiny and recovery of ₹2.09 crore. The pending 181 cases (₹45.22 crore) include 
three waiver cases and two cancelled -registration cases, as suppor ting documents were not 
furnished. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
82 | P a g e  
4.6.1.5 (a) Cancellation of registrations with effect from dates preceding date of 
application  
Rule 22(3) of the HPGST Rules provides that where a person who has submitted an 
application for cancellation (REG -16) of his registration is no longer liable to be 
registered or his registration liable to be cancelled,  the Proper Officer shall cancel the 
registration with effect from a date to be determined by him and notify the taxable 
person, directing him to pay arrears of any tax, interest or penalty including the amount 
liable to be paid under Sub -section (5) of Section 29. In any cas e, the effective d ate 
should not be a date earlier than the date of application for the same. 
Audit observed (November 2023-January 2024) across 10 Circles that in 403 out of 
656 cases of cancellation at taxpayer’s request during 2020-21, the date of cancellation 
preceded the date of application (Appendix-4.3). This is violation of Rule 22(3) 
indicating system validation failure and is fraught with the risk of irregular utilisation 
of ITC, evasion of tax, interest and penalty.  
On this being pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024), the Department forwarded 
(June 2025)  the replies of four Circles wherein Cart Road Circle stated that all 
formalities had been done effectively; Nalagarh -II Circle stated that the  monitoring 
mechanism would be made effective for future task  and two19 Circles stated that the 
cancellation was done at the request of taxpayers.  
4.6.1.5 (b) Delays in cancellation 
The cancellation order in REG -19 has to be issued within 30 days from the date of 
application (taxpayers request) or the date of reply to REG -17 in case of suo moto  
cancellation.  
Out of 1,438 cases of cancellation (656 cases on taxpayers request and 782 cases of suo 
moto cancellation), Audit noticed that in 684 cases (319 cases on taxpayers request and 
365 cases of suo moto cancellation) falling under 10 Circles, the cancellations were 
delayed beyond the stipulated period  of 30 days with delay  ranging from 1 day to 
953 days (delay in cancellation ranged from 1 to 30 days in 183 cases and from 31 to 
953 days in 501 cases). The details of such cases are brought out in Appendix-4.4. 
On this being pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024), the Department forwarded 
(June 2025) the replies of four Circles20 wherein it was inter alia  stated that the 
cancellations were delayed due to time granted to taxpayers to fulfil statutory 
obligations or  time required to verify the outstanding GST dues/liabilities pending 
against the taxpayers.  
The reply of the Department is not satisfactory as in majority of the cases the delay in 
cancellation was more than 31 days and in some cases it was more than two years.

20  Baddi-I; Barotiwala; Cart Road; Nalagarh-II.

83 | P a g e  
4.6.1.5 (c) Inadequate follow up on non-filing of GSTR 10 
As per Section 45 of the HPGST Act, GSTR-10 – the final return, has to be filed within 
three months of the effective date of cancellation or the date of order of cancellation, 
whichever is later.  
As per the HPGST Rules, 2017 and as prescribed in Circular No. 129/48/2019 -GST 
dated 24 December 2019, GSTR-3A has to be issued to the taxpayer, where GSTR-10 
has not been filed. If the taxpayer still fails to file the final return within 15 days of the 
receipt of notice, then an assessment order in Form ASMT -13 under Section 62 of the 
HPGST Act , read with R ule 100 of the HPGST Rules , shall have to be issued to 
determine the liability of the taxpayer under Sub -section (5) of Section 29 ( i.e., debit 
ITC equivalent to inputs, and inputs contained in semi-finished and finished goods held 
in stock or capital goods or the output tax payable on such goods, whichever is higher). 
If the taxpayer files the final return within 30 days from the issue of order ASMT -13, 
then the said order shall be deemed to have been withdrawn. However, the liability for 
payment of interest and late fee shall continue.  If the said return remains unfurnished 
within the statutory period of 30 days from the issue of order ASMT-13, then the Proper 
Officer may initiate proceedings under Section 78 and recovery under Section 79 of the 
HPGST Act. 
Audit observed that GSTR-10 was not filed in 613 cases out of 1 ,438 cases of 
cancellations during 2020 -21 across 10 Circles. Further the action  initiated against 
those who did not comply even after rece iving such notices in the above -mentioned 
cases was not found on record (Appendix-4.5). 
On this being pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024), the Department forwarded 
(June 2025) the replies of five Circles21 wherein Mandi-I Circle stated that in 10 cases 
GSTR 10 has been filed  and Nalagarh-II Circle stated that in some cases A SMT-13 
could not be issued as the taxpayers  had not filed any GSTR-1,3B or 9, hence, tax 
liability could not be assessed.  
Recommendation 4: 
Rectify Delays in Registration Cancellation and Enforce Final Return Filing:  
Install system-based validation checks to prevent cancellation dates from preceding 
application dates. Track pendency of cancellation applications and final return 
(GSTR-10) filing, and initiate enforcement action in cases of default. 
4.6.1.6 Not filing of GST Form ITC-03 by taxpayers who opted for Composition 
Scheme 
As per Section 10 of the HPGST Act , read with Rule 3 and Rule 44 of HPGST Rules, 
any taxpayer opting for composition scheme has to file GST Form ITC -03 within 180 
days, for reversal of credit lying in ledger preceding the day on which registration under 
composition scheme was obtained. 
 
21  Baddi-I; Barotiwala; Cart Road; Mandi-I; Nalagarh-II.  

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
84 | P a g e  
During scrutiny of MIS report available on GST Portal of all 10 Circles, it was observed 
that in 34 cases, under five Circles22, taxpayers opted for composition scheme during 
the period 2020-21, but did not file GST Form ITC-03. The total ITC of ₹2.35 lakh was 
available in their ledgers and was not reversed (Appendix-4.6). 
On this being pointed out (December 2023), the Department forwarded (June 2025) the 
reply of Cart Road Circle which stated that in case  of one taxpayer , no ITC was 
available with the taxpayers when he opted the composition scheme . The reply is not 
acceptable as it can be seen in the electronic credit ledger (ECL) that an ITC of 
₹10,808/- was available. It is pertinent to mention that not just the reversal of unutilised 
ITC available in the electronic credit ledger, the non -filing of ITC -03 is also fraught 
with the probable risk of non-reversal of utilised ITC on the unsold stock lying with 
taxpayers.  
Recommendation 5: 
Mandate ITC Reversals in Composition Scheme Transitions: Monitor and enforce 
filing of Form ITC-03 by taxpayers opting for the Composition Scheme. Circles must 
be sensitised to identify such cases and ensuring reversal of ITC within 180 days as 
mandated. 
4.6.1.7 Not verifying of DRC-03 resulting in misclassification of tax paid 
Form DRC-03 is a digital intimation of payment made by taxpayer voluntarily or made 
against show cause notice/statement as per Section 73(7) and S ection 74(7), the tax 
officer is expected to verify the payment received from taxpayer and if the amount paid 
falls short of the amount actually payable, he shall proceed to issue the notice in respect 
of such amount which falls short of the amount actually payable and DRC 03 is 
displayed on the dashboard of jurisdictional Circle officer who in turn verifies himself 
or re-assign to the Proper Officer depending upon monetary limit, since the verification 
of DRC-03 falls in the ambit of adju dication. The acknowledgement is issued in form 
DRC-04, and the conclusion of proceedings is intimated through DRC-05. 
As per information available on GST portal, Audit observed that DRC -03 was 
submitted by the taxpayers in 945 cases across 10 Circles during 2020-21. Out of these, 
778 cases were shown pending for verification at the end of the Proper Officer level 
and an amount of ₹17.32 crore was remitted by taxpayers in these cases. Audit noticed 
that in 153 out of 778 cases,  an amount of ₹ three crore was unequally deposited 
between CGST and SGST Heads. In 37 out of 153 cases, an amount of ₹ 1.63 crore was 
deposited in excess in CGST Head instead of depositing 50 percent i.e., ₹ 0.82 crore in 
SGST Head resulting into loss of revenue to the State (Appendix-4.7). This can lead to 
inaccuracies in financial statements, potentially impacting the State/Center 
performance and revenue recognition. 
 
22 1: Cart Road; 2: Mandi-I; 3: Nahan-I; 4: Sanjauli; 5: Solan-I 

Chapter IV: Department’s Oversight on GST Payments and Returns Filing for the Year 2018-19 to 2020-21 
 
85 | P a g e  
On this being pointed out (December 2023 to March 2025), the Department forwarded 
(June 2025) the replies of five Circles23 wherein three Circles24 stated that DRC-03 has 
been verified, acknowledged and no discrepancy or miscalculation was noticed and two 
Circles25 stated that the task bar was unabled. 
The reply is not satisfactory as though the whole payment of tax was made but due to 
misclassification the State government was deprived of GST due to crediting the same 
to CGST head. The remaining five Circles did not furnish the replies. 
4.6.1.8  Not issuing of adjudication orders for Show Cause Notices issued under 
Section 73 
As per section 73(10)  of HPGST Act , the Proper Officer shall issue the order under 
Sub-section (9) within three years from the due date for furnishing of annual return for 
the financial year to which the tax was not paid or short paid or input tax credit was 
wrongly availed or utilised relates to or within three years from the date of erroneous 
refund.  
During scrutiny of MIS report s available on GST portal, it was  observed that during 
2020-21, Show Cause Notices (SCNs) under Section 73 pertaining to Assessment Year 
2017-18 were issued in 34 cases across five Circles26. 
As per Section 73(9) of HPGST Act, the Proper Officer should have after considering 
the representation, if any, made by person chargeable with tax, determine the amount 
of tax, interest and a penalty equivalent to ten per cent of tax or ₹10,000, whichever is 
higher, due from such person and issue an order.  The time limit to issue the order for 
returns pertaining to Assessment Year 2017 -18 was December 2023. However, it was 
observed (November 2023) that orders were not issued in the said cases. This is fraught 
with the risk of forgo ing revenue amounting to ₹ 0.96 crore as no order can be issued 
against these taxpayers after the prescribed time limit (Appendix-4.8).  
The reply of the Department was awaited (June 2025). 
Recommendation 6: 
Issue Time-Bound SCN Disposal Protocol: Develop and enforce a protocol for timely 
issuance of orders against SCNs under Section 73 to avoid loss of revenue due to 
expiry of limitation periods and integrate this protocol within the Department’s MIS 
system.

accordance with the provisions of this Act but fails to pay the tax or any part thereof to 
the Government within the period prescribed , shall for the period for which the tax

4.6.1.10 Suppression of taxable value identified through TDS declaration 
As per Section 61 of the HPGST Act, various returns filed by taxpayers have to be 
scrutinised by the Proper Officer to verify the correctness of the returns, and suitable 
action has to be taken on any discrepancies or inconsistencies reflected in the returns.  
Audit obtained the data of taxpayers from MIS report on GSTN Portal, who accepted 
TDS credit and filed nil GSTR 3B returns, and cross verified the TDS deducted month 
wise with  GSTR 3B. It was noticed that during 2020 -21, TDS of ₹2.43 crore was 
accepted by 323 taxpayers vide 569 returns across 10 Circles, whereas in the monthly 
returns GSTR 3B, these taxpayers have shown nil taxable turnover. This indicates a 
probable suppression of the taxable turnover of ₹121.33 crore (TDS*100/2) involving 
GST liability to the tune of ₹21.84 crore worked out by applying tax rate of 18 per cent 
(Appendix-4.9). 
On this being  pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024),  the Department stated 
(June 2025) that additional demand in three cases for ₹2.52 crore  has been created by 
Cart Road Circle and notices have been issued in eight cases  by two Circles27. 
Remaining replies were awaited (June 2025).  
4.7 Limited Audit 
Audit analysed GST returns data pertaining to 2018 -19 to 2020-21 as made available 
by GSTN. Rule-based deviations and logical inconsistencies between GST returns filed 
by taxpayers were identified on a set of 16 parameters for the purpose of Limited Audit.  
Out of the 13 prescribed GST returns28, the following basic returns that apply to normal 
taxpayers were considered for the purpose of identifying deviations, inconsistencies 
and mismatches between GST returns/data: 
 
27  Mandi-I and Nalagarh-II 
28  GSTR-1; GSTR-3B; GSTR-4 (taxpayers under the Composition scheme); GSTR-5 (non-resident 
taxable person); GSTR -5A (Non -resident OIDAR service providers); GSTR -6 (Input service 
distributor); GSTR -7 (taxpayers deducting TDS); GSTR -8 (E -commerce operator); GSTR -9 
(Annual Return); GSTR-10 (Final return); GSTR-11 (person having UIN and claiming a refund);

Chapter IV: Department’s Oversight on GST Payments and Returns Filing for the Year 2018-19 to 2020-21 
 
87 | P a g e  
▪ GSTR-1: monthly return furnished by all normal and casual registered taxpayers 
making outward supplies of goods and services or both and contains details of 
outward supplies of goods and services. 
▪ GSTR-3B: monthly summary return of outward supplies and input tax credit 
claimed, along with payment of tax by the taxpayer to be filed by all taxpayers 
except those specified under Section 39(1) of the Act. This is the return that 
populates the credit and debits in the ECL and debits in Electronic Cash Ledger. 
▪ GSTR-6: monthly return for Input Service Distributors providing the details of 
their distributed input tax credit and inward supplies. 
▪ GSTR-8: monthly return to be filed by the e-commerce operators who are required 
to deduct TCS (Tax collected at source) under GST, introduced in October 2018. 
▪ GSTR-9: annual return to be filed by all registered persons other than an Input 
Service Distributor (ISD), Tax Deductor at Source/Tax Collector at Source, Casual 
Taxable Person and Non-Resident taxpayer. This document contains the details of 
all supplies made and received under various tax heads (CGST, HPGST and IGST) 
during the entire year along with turnover and audit details for the same.  
▪ GSTR-9C: annual audit form for all taxpayers having a turnover above rupees 
five crore in a particular financial year.  It is basically  a reconciliation statement 
between the annual returns filed in GSTR -9 and the taxpayer's audited annual 
financial statements. 
▪ GSTR-2A: a system -generated statement of inward supplies for a recipient.  It 
contains the details of all Business to Business (B2B) transactions of suppliers 
declared in their Form GSTR -1 / 5, ISD details from GSTR 6, details from  
GSTR-7 and GSTR- 8 respectively by the counterparty and import of goods from 
overseas on bill of entry, as received from ICEGATE Portal of Indian Customs.

Tables 4.2 (a) and (b).

4.7.1 Non-submission of reply by the department 
Audit selected a sample of 627 deviations pertaining to  348 taxpayers from the top 
deviations/inconsistencies across 16 parameters for the years 2018-19 to 2020-21. The 
audit queries were issued to the respective Circles in July 2023 without further scrutiny 
of taxpayer’s records. The audit check in these cases was limited to verifying the 
Department’s action on the identified deviations/mismatches.  
Responses were yet to be received in respect of  207 deviations (33.01 percent), as of 
June 2025, in 13 parameters communicated to the Department (March 2024). 
Considering that the overall rate of conversion of inconsistencies into compliance 
deviations is significant as brought out in the next paragraph, the Department is required 
to expedite verification of these cases on priority. Details of the cases  where replies 
have not been received are listed in Appendix-4.11 & 4.12.

4.7.3 Summary of Limited Audit 
ITC and tax payment mismatch (Appendix-4.13) 
Out of the total deviations/mismatch in 578 cases, the Department furnished replies in 
389 cases (67.30 per cent ) involving an amount of ₹751.80 crore . Out of these 
389 cases, recovery ha s been affected  in 15 cases amounting to ₹ 3.69 crore 
(Appendix-4.13). In 47 and 62 cases, DRC-07 and SCNs have been issued amounting 
to ₹66.42 crore and  ₹297.24 crore respectively (Appendix-4.15 and 4.16). In 137 
cases, the matter is under correspondence with taxpayer amounting to ₹ 187.48 crore 
(Appendix-4.17). In 41 cases, the Department replies were furnished without 
documentary evidence amounting to ₹58.14 crore (Appendix-4.18).  
Data entry e rrors by taxpayers comprised eight  cases amounting to ₹24.16  crore 
(Appendix-4.19), action had already been taken by the Department in 34  cases 
amounting to ₹37.84 crore (Appendix-4.20) and in 45 cases amounting to ₹64.93 crore 
explanations for mismatches were found valid (Appendix-4.21). 
Turnover cases (Appendix-4.22) 
Similarly, Audit also noticed mismatches in turnover reported by taxpayers in 49 cases 
out of which Department furnished replies in 31 cases amounting to ₹6,350.31 crore. 
Out o f these,  the Department a ffected recovery of ₹0.03 crore  in one  case 
(Appendix-4.14) and DRC-07 had been issued in three  cases for ₹3.15 crore  
(Appendix-4.15). SCN has been issued in four cases for ₹21.55 crore  
(Appendix-4.16). In three cases, matter was under correspondence with taxpayer  for 
₹11.30 crore  (Appendix-4.17) and i n 13 cases, the Department ’s replies were 
furnished without documentary evidence amounting to ₹5 ,473.40 crore  
(Appendix-4.18).  
Action had already been taken in one case for ₹3.38 crore (Appendix-4.20) and 
six cases had valid explanation for mismatch (₹829.39 crore) (Appendix-4.21).  
Relatively higher rates of deviations were not iced in risk parameters such as 
non-payment of interest, cases where there was a mismatch between ITC available in 
GSTR-2A and ITC availed through GSTR -3B, suppression of taxable value (E -Way 
Bill), undisclosed taxable turnover (Table 7G) etc. 
Illustrative cases of each Dimension are discussed below:  
 
(i) Dimension – ITC Mismatch (R3B & R2A) 
GSTR 2A is a purchase related dynamic tax return that is automatically generated for 
each business by the GST portal, whereas GSTR 3B is a monthly return in which 
summary of outward supplies along with ITC declared and payment of tax are 
self-declared by the taxpayer.  
To analyse the veracity of ITC utilisation, relevant data were extracted from GSTR 3B 
and GSTR 2A for the years 2018 -19 to 2020 -21, and the ITC paid as per suppliers’ 
details was matched with the ITC credit availed by the taxpayer. The methodology 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
90 | P a g e  
adopted was to compare the ITC available as per GSTR 2A with all its amendments 
and the ITC availed in GSTR 3B in Table 4A (5) 29 considering the reversals in Table 
4B (2)30 but including the ITC availed in the respective subsequent years from Table 
8C of GSTR 9.  
Audit observed that in case of taxpayer31 under Nurpur circle, the ITC available as per 
GSTR 2A was ₹0.013 crore and the ITC availed in table 4A (5) of GSTR 3B was 
₹18.83 crore. This resulted in mismatch of ITC availed amounting to ₹18.82 crore.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied (June 2025) that demand 
order in DRC-07 for ₹18.82 crore has been issued in May 2024. 
(ii)  Dimension - ITC Availed without supplier remitting tax 
While GSTR 2A of the recipient is generated based on the disclosures made by the 
suppliers in their GSTR 1, the Table 8A of the GSTR 9 is auto-populated (non-editable) 
from Tables 3, 4, 5 and 6 of the GSTR 2A (Tables 3 and 5 of the old GSTR 2A format) 
and considers GSTR 2A as available on 31 October of the following financial year. 
In order to analy se the extent of compliance of ITC availment under Section 16(4) of 
the HPGST Act, Audit identified cases where the ITC would have been passed on by 
the taxpayer without actually remitting the tax. For this purpose, the relevant data from 
GSTR 9 particularly pertaining to table 8A of GSTR 9 was compared with the ITC data 
reflected in GSTR 2As. 
Audit observed that  in case of a taxpayer32 under Baddi -III circle, the ITC as per 
GSTR 2A was ₹67.11 crore whereas ITC reflected in table 8A of GSTR 9 for availment 
was ₹64.70 crore. This indicates that the taxpayer availed ITC of ₹2.41 crore on which 
supplier had not remitted the tax.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied (June 2025) that demand 
order has been issued to the taxpayer.  
(iii)  Dimension - ITC Availed on 3B filed after limitation period 
GST Act provides for availing of ITC only till the due date of furnishing GSTR 3B for 
the month of September following the end of the financial year to which such ITC 
pertains or furnishing of relevant Annual Return, whichever is earlier. Accordingly, if 
any GSTR 3B is furnished after such time, ITC availed therein becomes inadmissible. 
In order to review the extent of excess/irregular ITC availed on this account, the ITC 
availed through Table 4 of GSTR 3Bs pertaining to 2018-19, 2019-20 and 2020-21 filed 
by the taxpayer beyond the due dates of September GSTR 3B return of the following 
year, were identified at data level.

(iv)  Dimension - Excess ISD credit 
In order to analy se whether the ITC reversals made by the ISD in their GSTR 6 have 
been rightly reversed by the respective recipients of the credit, with the centralised data 
available in the GSTN, the adjustments made by the ISD in their GSTR 6 are compared 
with the ITC reversals of the recipients declared in their annual return.  
The GSTR 9 Table 6G of the respective recipients after taking into account maximum 
reversals through Table 7B and 7H of GSTR 9 was  compared with sum of Table 5A, 
8A and 9A of GSTR 6s of the ISD for the respective GSTINs  after including all 
adjustments made by the ISDs . In cases where GSTR 9 is not filed, corresponding 
figures of GSTR 3B have been taken into account. 
Audit observed that in case of a taxpayer34 under Baddi-IV Circle, ITC availed through 
ISD as per GSTR 9 was ₹ 1.81 crore in 2018 -19 and 2019 -20 whereas the ISD 
distributed ITC of only ₹0.15 crore through GSTR 6 and reversal of ₹1.12 crore was 
made by recipient in Table 7B/7H of GSTR 9. This resulted in availment of excess ISD 
credit of ₹0.54 crore.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied ( June 2025 ) that 
ASMT-10 has been issued to taxpayer. 
(v)  Dimension - Short payment of tax on RCM 
Under R everse Charge Mechanism (RCM) the liability to pay tax is fixed on the 
recipient of supply of goods or services instead of the supplier or provider in respect of 
certain categories of goods or services or both under Section 9(3) or Section 9(4) of the 
HPGST Act, 2017 and under Sub -section (3) or Sub -section (4) of Section 5 of the 
IGST Act, 2017. 
To analyse the veracity of ITC availed on tax paid under RCM for the year 2017 -18, 
the datasets pertaining to GSTR 3B and annual return GSTR 9 were compared to check 
whether the ITC availed on RCM was restricted to the extent of tax paid. The 
methodology adopted was to compare the RCM payments in GSTR 3B Table 3.1( d)35 
with ITC availed in GSTR 9 Table 6C 36, 6D37 and 6F38. In cases where GSTR 9 was 
not available, the check was restricted within GSTR 3B where the tax discharged part

is nil.   
(vi)  Dimension - Unreconciled ITC w.r.t. Financial Statements (Table 12F) 
Table 12 of GSTR 9C reconciles ITC declared in annual return (GSTR9) with ITC 
availed as per audited Annual financial statement or books of accounts. Column 12F of 
this table deals with unreconciled ITC. 
The certified reconciliation statements submitted by the taxpayer as required under the 
Rule 80(3) of HPGST Rules in form GSTR 9C for the years 2018 -19 to 2020-21 were 
analysed at data level to review the extent of identified mismatch in ITC declared in the 
Annual Return with the Financial Statements.

taxpayer42 under Jawali Circle, was noticed.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied (June 2025) that DRC-07 
has been issued to the taxpayer. 
(vii)  Dimension - Ineligible ITC (Table 14T) 
Table 14 of GSTR 9C reconciles ITC declared in annual return (GSTR9) with ITC 
availed on expenses as per audited Annual financial statement or books of accounts.

(viii) Dimension - Undischarged tax liability (R1 & R9) 
In order to analy se the undischarged tax liability, relevant data were extracted from 
GSTR 1, and GSTR 9 for the years 2018 -19 to 2020-21, and the tax payable in these 
returns was compared with the tax paid declared in GSTR 9. Where GSTR 9 was not 
available, a comparison of tax payable between GSTR 1 and GSTR 3B was resorted to. 
The amendments and advance adjustments declared in GSTR 1 and 9 were also 
considered for this purpose. 
For the algorithm, tables 4 to 11 of GSTR 1 and tables 4G, 4N, 10, and 11 of GSTR 9 
were considered. The greater of tax liability between GSTR 1 and GSTR 9 was 
compared with the tax paid declared in tables 9 and 14 of GSTR 9 to identify the short 
payment of tax. In the case of GSTR 3B, tables 6.1 minus table 3.1(d) were taken into 
account. 
Audit observed that in case of a taxpayer44 under jurisdiction of Baddi-II circle, the 
greater of tax liability between GSTR 1 and GSTR 9 was ₹3.02 crore in 2019-20 and 
2020-21 whereas tax paid as per GSTR 3B was ₹1.88 crore resulting in undischarged 
tax liability of ₹1.14 crore.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied (June 2025) that demand 
has been created in March 2024 for ₹1.14 crore. 
(ix) Dimension - Suppression of taxable value (E-Way Bill) 
In order to analyse the extent of short -payment of tax, relevant data related to tax 
liability declared in GSTR 3B for the years 2018 -19 to 2020-21 were compared with 
disclosures made in E-way bill. 
For the algorithm, the ca ses where tax payable under outward taxable supplies other 
than nil rated and exempted in GSTR 3B was  less than the tax liability declared in the 
e-way bills were chosen. 
Audit observed that in case of a taxpayer45 under jurisdiction of Nalagarh-II circle, tax 
payable under outward taxable supplies other than nil rated and exempted in GSTR 3B 
for years 2018 -19 to 2020 -21 was  ₹1,099.70 crore whereas tax liability declared in 
various e-way bills generated in the said years was ₹ 1,284.63 crore resulting in non -
payment of tax on outward supply of ₹184.93 crore.

Deduction at Source (TDS) / Tax collection at Source (TCS) declaration

communicated to the registered person in Table 9 of GSTR -2A. The cases where the 
taxable value declared on account of outward taxable supplies (other than zero rated, 
nil rated and exempted) in GSTR 3B was less than the net amount liable for TCS and 
TDS credit as per Table 9 of GSTR -2A were identified. Though the deviation amount 
indicated may only be a fraction of non-disclosure, exact comparison is not possible as 
the taxpayer may have other supplies which do not necessitate TDS recovery.  Hence, 
Department was requested to examine all the transactions and not to limit checks to the 
deviation amount.

value of TDS credit as per table 9 of GSTR 2A was ₹0.87 crore which tantamount to 
taxable supplies of ₹43.54 crore worked out on the criteria that two percent tax has been 
deducted at source . This indicates a probable suppression of taxable value of 
₹13.89 crore on account of TDS declaration.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied ( June 2025) that 
ASMT-10 has been issued to the taxpayer.

available 
Audit identified those taxpayers who had not filed GSTR 3B but filed GSTR 1 or whose

liability is offset and ITC is availed. The very availability of GSTR 1 and 2A and non-
filing of R3B indicates that the taxpayers had undertaken/carried on the business during 
the period but have not discha rged their tax liability. This is fraught with the risk of 
irregular passing on of ITC.  
The datasets pertaining to relevant fields in the GSTR 1, 2A and 3B were analysed for 
the years 2018 -19 to 2020 -21, and those cases where GSTR3B is null have been 
extracted.

GSTR 3B returns for the months from May to December in 2018-19 and the tax liability 
as per GSTR 1 for the said months was ₹17.63 crore. Thus, tax liability of ₹17.63 crore 
had not been discharged by the taxpayer.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied (June 2025) that the case 
is under correspondence with taxpayer. 
(xvi) Dimension – Non-payment of interest 
Section 50 of the Act stipulates that every person liable to pay tax in accordance with 
the provisions of this Act or the rules made there under but fails to pay the tax or any 
part thereof to the Government within the period prescribed, shall for the peri od for 
which the tax or any part thereof remains unpaid, pay interest at the rate notified. 
The extent of short payment of interest on account of delayed remittance of tax during 
2018-19 to 2020-21 was identified using the tax paid details in GSTR 3B and the date 
of filing of the GSTR 3B. Only the net tax liability (cash component) has been 
considered to work out the interest payable.

4.7.4 Analysis of causative factors 
Considering the Department’s response to 420 queries out of the sample of total 627 
queries issued, the factors that caused the data deviations/inconsistencies are as follows: 
a. Cases accepted by the Department: 
Out of the 420 cases involving ₹710 3.11 crore summari sed in para 4 .7.3 
(Appendix 4.13 and 4.22) above, the Department has recovered ₹3.72 crore in 16 cases 
and raised demand for ₹69.57 crore in 50 cases. Further, the Department has issued 
SCN/DRC-01/ASMT-10 Notice in 66 cases for ₹318.79 crore; and is under 
correspondence with taxpayers in 140 cases involving ₹198.78 crore. Also, the 
Department had already taken action before issue of audit query in 35 cases involving 
₹41.22 crore. 
Illustration of Data Entry Error: 
The data entry errors constituted 1.90 per cent  (eight cases) of the total responses 
received and the Department’s responses in these cases were accepted by Audit.  
Audit observed that in case of taxpayer 53 under jurisdiction of Nalagarh -I circle, the 
taxpayer as per table s 6C, 6D and 6F of GSTR 9 , had an inward supply from an 
unregistered supplier on which it was liable to pay tax under RCM amounting to 
₹53.05 lakh whereas tax paid under RCM in table 4G of GSTR 9 was ₹0.01 lakh. This 
resulted in short payment of tax amounting to ₹53.04 lakh.  
On this being pointed out (July 2023), the Department replied (June 2025) that the 
taxpayer has wrongly entered the figure of ITC claimed of ₹53.05 lakh in table 6(D) 
instead of table 6 (B) of GSTR9 for the year 2020-21 and the taxpayer has not claimed 
excess ITC under RCM. The explanation provided was accepted in audit. 
b. Cases where Department’s reply is not acceptable to Audit:  
The Department’s reply was not acceptable to Audit in 54 cases (41 ITC and Tax 
payment mismatch cases + 13 Turnover mismatch cases ) involving an amount of 
₹5531.53 crore (₹58.14 crore + ₹5473.40 crore) . The reasons for not accepting the 
replies of the Department were documentary evidence were not provided, cases already 
flagged in Economic Intelligence Unit  (EIU) reports, no valid explanations were 
furnished, cases requested to be dropped without verifying the facts of the case and 
investigation.

4.8 Detailed Audit  
In a self-assessment regime, the onus of compliance with law is on the taxpayer. The 
role of the Department is to establish and maintain an efficient tax administration 
mechanism to provide oversight. With a finite level of resources, for an effective tax 
administration, to ensure compliance with law and collection of revenue, an efficient 
governance mechanism is essential. An IT driven compliance model enables 
maintaining a non-discretionary regime of governance on scale and facilitates a targeted 
approach to enforce compliance.  
From an external audit perspective, Audit also focused on a data -driven risk -based 
approach. Thus, apart from identifying inconsistencies/deviations in GST returns 
through pan-India data analysis of State jurisdiction taxpayers, a detailed audit of GST 
returns was also conducted as a part of this review. A risk-based sample of 50 taxpayers 
was selected for this part of the review. The methodology adopted was to initially 
conduct a desk review of GST returns and financial statements filed by the taxpayers 
as part of the GSTR 9C and other records available in the back -end system to identify 
potential risk areas, inconsistencies/deviations and red flags. Desk review was carried 
out in CAG field audit offices. Based on desk review re sults, detailed audit was 
conducted in selected Circles of the state by requisitioning corresponding granular 
records of taxpayers such as financial ledgers, invoices etc. to identify causative factors 
of the identified risks and to evaluate compliance by taxpayers. 
As brought out in the previous paragraphs detailed audit involved a desk review of GST 
returns and other basic records to identify risks and red flags, which were followed up 
by field audit to identify the extent of non-compliance by taxpayers and action taken by 
the concerned Circles. Non -compliance by taxpayers at various stages ultimately 
impacts the veracity of returns filed, utilisation of ITC and discharge of tax payments. 
The audit findings are therefore categorised under i) Returns, ii) Utilisation of ITC and 
iii) Discharge of tax liability. 
 
 
54  GSTIN- 02XXXXXXXXXX1ZA

4.8.1  Scope limitation (non-production/partial production of records) 
There was scope limitation of audit due to non -production of granular record s by the 
Department. During the desk review of taxpayers’ returns available in the back -end 
system, Audit identified the risks related to excess ITC and tax liability mismatches for 
detailed examination. On the ITC dimension, the mismatches were identified by 
comparing GSTR 3B with GSTR 2A and GSTR 9, and the declarations made in Table 
12 and 14 of GSTR 9C. On the tax liability dimension, the mismatches were identified 
by comparing GSTR 3B with GSTR 1 and GSTR 9 and the declarations in Table 5, 
Table 7, and Table 9 of GSTR 9C. However, the Department did not provide the 
corresponding granular records such as the supplementary financial ledgers, invoices, 
agreement copies etc. required for examining the causative factors for mismatches of 
ITC and tax liability in 29 cases constituting 58 per cent of the sample size 
(Appendix-4.23) and in remaining 21 cases (Appendix-4.24) the related record viz., 
supplementary financial ledgers, invoices agreement copies etc. were produced 
partially. Due to non-production/partial production of records the identified risk could 
not be examined in detail by the Audit and audit observations were limited to 
mismatches of ITC and tax liability as discussed in the respective paras. 
4.8.2  Returns 
The detailed audit of returns filed by the sample of 50 taxpayers disclosed that interest 
payments were not discharged by taxpayers which are brought out below: 
Non-payment of interest by taxpayers  
Audit observed in 11 cases of nine Circles, constituting 22 per cent of the 50 cases 
audited, that taxpayers had filed their returns belatedly, but the interest payments  
amounting to ₹3.14 lakh were not discharged.  
On this being  pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024),  the Department  replied 
(June 2025) that ₹1.69 lakh had been recovered in four cases, demand order has been 
issued in one case  and notices have been issued in two cases.  The reply in remaining 
cases was not furnished (Appendix-4.25).  
4.8.3  Utilisation of Input Tax Credit  
Input Tax Credit (ITC) means the Goods and Services Tax (GST) paid by a taxable 
person on purchase of goods and/or services that are used in the course or furtherance 
of business. To avoid cascading effect of taxes, credit of taxes paid on input supplies 
can be used to set-off for payment of taxes on outward supplies. 
Sections 16 and 17 of the HPGST Act prescribe the eligibility and conditions to avail 
ITC. Credit of CGST cannot be used for payment of SGST and credit of SGST cannot 
be utilised for payment of CGST. Rules 36 to 45 of the HPGST Rules prescribes the 
procedures for availing and reversal of ITC. 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
100 | P a g e  
(a) Non reversal/Short reversal of ITC  
Section 17(2) of the HPGST Act read with Rule 42 of the HPGST Rules states that 
where the goods or services or both are used by the registered person partly for effecting 
taxable supplies including zero-rated supplies and partly for effecting exempt supplies, 
the amount of credit shall be restricted to so much of the input tax as is attributable to 
the said taxable supplies including zero-rated supplies. 
Audit observed non -compliance in four cases of four Circles out of 50 cases where 
taxpayers had not reversed ITC of ₹55.20 lakh, due to incorrect application of Rule 42 
of HPGST Rules.  
On this being  pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024), the Department  stated 
(June 2025) that notice has been issued in one case (Appendix-4.26). 
An illustrative case is featured below: 
In one case 55, the taxpayer  under Nahan -II circle  had written off an amount of 
₹1.03 crore in the financial statements for the year 2019-20 on account of loss of goods 
in fire. Though the said stock was claimed to have been burnt, the ITC availed on them 
was not reversed either in the monthly returns or in the annual returns of the taxpayer. 
This resulted in non-reversal of ITC amounted to ₹18.58 lakh (worked out at the rate of 
18 per cent as per rate of maximum purchases of GSTR 2A).  
The reply of the Department was awaited (June 2025).  
(b) Other observations relating to discharge of tax liabilities where additional 
records were not provided and only mismatch details through data 
analysis could be observed are summarised below:

4.8.4 Discharge of tax liability 
The taxable event in case of GST is supply of goods and/or services. Section 9 of the 
HPGST Act is the charging Section authori sing levy and collection of tax called 
Central/Himachal Pradesh Goods and Services Tax on all intra-State supplies of goods 
or services or both, except on supply of alcoholic liquor for human consumption, on 
value determined under Section 15 of the Act ibid and at such rates not exceeding 
20 per cent under each Act, i.e., CGST Act and HPGST Act. Section 5 of the IGST 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
102 | P a g e  
vests levy and collection of IGST on interstate supply of goods and services with 
Central Government with maximum rate of 40 per cent.  
Under Section 8 of the Goods and Services Tax (Compensation to States) Act, 2017, a 
cess is levied on all inter-state and intra-state supply of such goods or services or both 
which are listed in the schedule of the said Act such as tobacco products, aerated drinks, 
cigarettes, vehicles etc. Section 9(4) of the HPGST Act and Sections 5(3) and 5(4) of 
the IGST Act provide for reverse charge levy on certain goods or services, wherein the 
recipient instead of supplier becomes liable to pay tax.  
The audit findings in this context are discussed below: 
a) Exclusion of supplies 
Section 7 of HPGST Act 2017 defines supplies to include all forms of supply of goods 
or services or both such as sale, transfer, barter, exchange, license, rental, lease or 
disposal made or agreed to be made for a consideration by a person in the course or  
furtherance of business. It also includes import of services for a consideration whether 
or not in the course or furtherance of business. This is an inclusive definition, main 
elements being (1) supply should be of goods or services, (2) supply has to be made for 
a consideration, (3) supply has to be made in the course or furtherance of business, 
(4) supply should be made by a taxable person, (5) supply should be a taxable supply, 
and (6) supply should be made within the taxable territory. Schedule I specifies certain 
activities which even made without a consideration shall be treated as supply. 
Schedule II specifies treatment of certain activities or transactions as either supply of 
goods or supply of services. Section 8 of HPGST Act 2017 deals with composition and 
mixed supplies. 
(i) Incorrect determination of value of supply  
Rule 15 of CGST Act, 2017 provides that the value of supply of goods or services or 
both shall be the transaction value, which is the price actually paid or payable for the 
supply of such goods or services, where the supplier and recipients are not related  and 
the price is the sole consideration for the supply. 
Scrutiny of the financial statements uploaded by two taxpayers under Baddi-I Circle for 
the years 2018-19 to 2020-21 revealed that the taxpayers had shown sale of fixed assets 
i.e., plant and machinery of ₹16.74 crore on which GST was liable to be paid at the 
price determinable under Section 15 ibid. The tax liability worked out at the 
conservative rate of 18 percent amounts to ₹3.01 crore.  
The reply of the Department was awaited (June 2025) (Appendix-4.35). 
(ii) Non/short payment of tax under Reverse Charge Mechanism 
As per the provisions of Section 9(3) of HPGST Act, 2017 and Section 5(3)of IGST 
Act, 2017, the Government may, on the recommendations of the GST Council, by 
notification, specify categories of supply of goods or services or both, the tax on which 
shall be paid on reverse charge basis by the recipient of such goods or services or both 
and all the provisions of this Act shall apply to such recipient as if he is the person liable 

Chapter IV: Department’s Oversight on GST Payments and Returns Filing for the Year 2018-19 to 2020-21

for paying the tax in relation to the supply of such goods or services or both. Further 
vide notification No. 4/17, 13/17 and 10/17 dated 28 -06-2017 notified the goods and 
services on which reverse charge is to be paid by the recipients of goods and servic es. 
Goods Transport Agency (GTA), legal and professional services and director’s 
remuneration fall under the categories of goods and services on which tax is to be paid 
by recipients under reverse charge mechanism. 
Audit observed com pliance deficiencies in 1 456 out of 50 cases  across 10 Circles 
amounting to ₹10.15 crore, due to taxpayers incorrectly discharging tax payments under 
the Reverse Charge Mechanism leading to short levy of tax.  
On this being pointed out (December 2023 to March 2024), the Department stated 
(June 2025) that notice had been issued in one case and one case is under 
correspondence with taxpayer (Appendix-4.36). 
An illustrative case is featured below: 
Scrutiny of the financial statements and annual return of taxpayer57 under Baddi-IV 
circle for the period 2018-19 to 2020-21 revealed that the taxpayer has shown freight 
and legal & professional expenses of ₹53.56  crore on which  payment of tax under 
Reverse Charge Mechanism (RCM)  of ₹7.71  crore was required to be paid as per 
expenses shown in profit and loss account. Audit cross -verified the figures of RCM 
paid by this taxpayer in GSTR 9 and noticed that taxpayer had paid tax of ₹2.55 crore 
resulting in short payment of tax under RCM to the tune of ₹5.16 crore.  
The reply of the Department was awaited (June 2025).  
(iii) Non-levy of Tax under RCM on Director remuneration  
As per Notification no.  13/2017 central tax(rate) dated 28 th June 2017 services 
provided by a Director of a company or a body corporate to the said company or body 
corporate are liable to pay GST under RCM. The rate of tax is applicable @ 18% w.e.f. 
1st July 2017. The remuneration paid to Director is not limited to only Director’s 
services as a Director. It also covers the services provided by the Director in any other 
capacity like consultant, advisor, etc.  
Scrutiny of financial statements of two taxpayers of two Circles58 for the years  
2018-19 to 2020-21 revealed that remuneration of ₹8.43 crore was paid to the Directors 
on which the tax under RCM at the rate of 18  per cent was required to be paid as this 
remuneration has been shown separately in the profit and loss account and not included 
under the Head - Salary and Wages. This resulted in non -levy of tax under RCM 
amounting to ₹1.52 crore.

Department may urgently pursue the 207 deviations identified through data analysis 
where replies were not furnished. Department may also initiate remedial measures 
for all other mismatches and compliance deficiencies pointed out in this report. 
4.9 Conclusion 
The Subject Specific Compliance Audit (SSCA) on Department Oversight on GST 
Payments and Return Filing (Phase-II) was undertaken with the objectives of assessing 
the adequacy of the system in monitoring return filing and tax payments, extent of 
compliance and Departmental oversight functions.  
This SSCA was predominantly based on data analysis, which highlighted risk areas, 
red flags and in some cases, rule -based deviations and logical inconsistencies in GST 
returns filed during the periods from 2018-19 to 2020-21. The SSCA entailed assessing 
the oversight functions of State jurisdictional formations at two levels – at the data level 
through data queries and at the functional level with a deeper detailed audit of both 
Circles and of the GST returns, which involved accessing taxpayer records.  
The audit sample therefore comprised 348 taxpayer’s sample data with high value 
inconsistencies across 16 parameters selected through data queries for Centralised 
Audit and 50 taxpayer’s sample data with high value inconsistencies selected on risk 
assessment for Detailed Audit of GST returns for three years.  
A review of the 10 Circles disclosed that Circle’s compliance to essential oversight 
functions, such as monitoring of returns filing, delay in issuing orders against show 
cause notices,  scrutiny of returns, audit, taxpayers’ compliance and cancellation of 
registration was inadequate. 
In Centralised Audit, 627 high value data inconsistencies were pursued, with revenue 
implication of ₹7671.67 crore. Relatively higher rates of deviations were noticed in 
non-payment of interest, cases where there was a mismatch between ITC available in 
GSTR-2A and ITC availed through GSTR -3B, suppression of taxable value (E -Way 
Bill), undisclosed taxable turnover (Table 7G) etc. 
Detailed Audit of GST returns also suggested significant non -compliance. Certain 
essential records such as supplementary financial ledgers, invoices, agreement copies 
etc. were not produced in 29 cases which resulted in scope limitation. The main 
deficiencies noticed were mismatch of ITC between GSTR -3B and GSTR -2A and 
mismatch of tax liability admitted in GSTR-1/GSTR-9 and tax paid shown in GSTR-9.  

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023  
106 | P a g e  
Considering the compliance deficiencies, pointed out in this report, the Department 
needs to reinforce the institutional mechanism in the field formations to establish and 
maintain effective oversight on return filing, taxpayer compliance, tax payments, 
cancellation of registrations and recovery of dues from defaulters. 
4.10  Recommendations: 
The Department may 
R-13. Establish a monitoring mechanism for prompt follow up of notices issued for 
non-filing and late-filing of GST returns and maintaining proper records of 
compliance.  
R-14. Institute a mechanism for timely scrutiny of returns under Section 61 and 
implement internal audit of registered taxpayers under Section 65 of the Act 
as a regular, risk-based function. Develop annual audit plans, maintain audit 
trails, and monitor follow -up actions on audit findings to ensure 
accountability.  
R-15. Develop a mechanism for tracking cases where DRC -07 has been issued. 
Mandate periodic reporting from field officers on recovery progress and 
enforce timely initiation of recovery proceedings under Section 79 in cases of 
non-compliance.  
R-16. Install system -based validation checks to prevent cancellation dates from 
preceding application dates. Track pendency of cancellation applications 
and final return (GSTR-10) filing, and initiate enforcement action in cases 
of default. 
R-17. Monitor and enforce filing of Form ITC -03 by taxpayers opting for the 
Composition Scheme. Circles must be sensitised to identify such cases and 
ensuring reversal of ITC within 180 days as mandated. 
R-18. Develop and enforce a protocol for timely issuance of orders against SCNs 
under Section 73 to avoid loss of revenue due to expiry of limitation periods 
and integrate this protocol within the Department’s MIS system. 
R-19. Urgently pursue the 207 deviations identified through data analysis where 
replies were not furnished. Department may also initiate remedial measures 
for all other mismatches and compliance deficiencies pointed out in this 
report.

Chapter V - Financial Performance of State Public Sector 
Enterprises (SPSEs) 
 
5.1  Summary of Financial Performance of State Public Sector Enterprises 
 
5.1.1 Introduction 
 
5.1.1.1 Purpose of the Report 
This Section of the Report discusses “the financial performance of Government 
Companies (GCs), Statutory Corporations (SCs) and Government Controlled Other 
Companies (GCOCs)”. 
5.1.1.2 Definition of Government Companies/Corporation 
A Government Company is defined in Section 2(45) of the Companies Act, 2013 as a 
company in which not less than 51  per cent of the paid -up share capital is held by 
Central Government, or by any State Government or Governments, or partly by the 
Central Government and partly by one or more State Governments and  includes a 
company which is a subsidiary of a Government Company. 
Besides, any other company1 owned or controlled, directly or indirectly, by the Central 
Government, or by any State Government  or Governments, or partly by the Central 
Government and partly by one or more State Governments are referred to in this Report 
as Government Controlled Other Companies. 
5.1.1.3 Mandate of Audit  
Audit of Government Companies and Government Controlled Other C ompanies is 
conducted by the Comptroller and Auditor General  of India (C&AG) under the 
provisions of Section 143(5) to 143(7) of the Companies Act  2013, read with Section 
19 of the CAG’s (Duties, Powers  and Conditions of Service) Act  1971 and the 
Regulations made thereunder.  Under the Companies Act, 2013, the CAG appoints 
Chartered Accountants as Statutory Auditors for Companies and gives directions on the 
manner in which the accounts are to be audited. In addition, CAG has the right to 
conduct a supplementary audit  of the Company’s financial statements . The statutes 
governing some Statutory Corporations require their accounts to be audited only by 
CAG. 
5.1.1.4 SPSEs and their contribution to the GSDP of the State 
SPSEs consist of State Government Companies , GCOCs and Statutory Corporations. 
SPSEs are established to carry out activities of commercial nature keeping in view the 
welfare of people and occupies  an important place in the State economy.  As on 
31 March 2023, there were 30 SPSEs in Himachal Pradesh  under the jurisdiction of 
CAG. These include  two 2  statutory Corporations, 24 Government Companies 
 
1 Companies (Removal of Difficulties) Seventh Order, 2014 issued by Ministry of Corporate 
Affairs vide Gazette Notification dated 4 September 2014. 
2 Himachal Pradesh Financial Corporation and Himachal Road Transport Corporation.

(including three  inactive3 government companies)  and four 4 Government Controlled 
Other Companies. The names of these SPSEs are given in Appendix 5.1.  
There are three inactive SPSEs (including one under liquidation) in the State.  These 
inactive SPSEs have investment of ₹79.79 crore , which includes Equity/capital 
investment of ₹19.64 crore (State Government: ₹17.75 crore and others: ₹1.89 crore) 
and State Government long -term loans of ₹60.15  crore. This is a critical area as the 
investments in inactive SPSEs do not contribute to the economic growth of the State. 
A ratio of turnover of SPSEs to  the Gross State Domestic Product (GSDP) shows the 
extent of activities of these SPSEs in the State economy.  The details of turnover of 
working SPSEs are given in Appendix 5.2. Turnover of working SPSEs and its ratio to

5.1.2.3 Adequacy of Assets 
Ratio of total debt/loans to total assets is one of the methods used to determine whether 
a Company can stay solvent. To be considered solvent, the value of an entity’s assets 
must be greater than the sum of its loans/debts. The coverage of long -term loans by 
value of total assets in working SPSEs (which had outstanding loans) as per their latest 
finalised accounts as on 30 September 2023 is given in Table 5.4.

5.1.4 Debt servicing and legal compliances 
 
5.1.4.1 Information on debt repayment and secretarial compliances for new 
debts secured and unsecured 
No repayment of debt was made during the period 2020-23 by the seven SPSEs having 
outstanding debts of ₹1,979.73 crore as on 30 September 2023. Another seven SPSEs 
have made debt repayments of ₹838.32 crore during 2020-23 out of their outstanding 
debt of ₹8,194.37 crore as detailed in Appendix 5.5. 
As per Section 77 (1) of the Companies Act 2013, it shall be the duty of every company 
creating a charge within or outside India, on its property or assets or any of its 
undertakings, whether tangible or otherwise, and situated in or outside India, to register 
the particulars of the charge signed by the company and the charge-holder together with 
the instruments, if any, creating such charge in such form, on payment of such fees and 
in such manner as may be prescribed, with the Registrar within thirty days of its 
creation. Section 2 (16) of the Act defines charge as an interest or lien created on the 
property or assets of a company or any of its undertakings or both as security and 
includes a mortgage.

charges with t he Registrar of Companies as required under  the provisions of the 
Companies Act. Registration of charge was not applicable to Himachal Road Transport 
Corporation and Himachal Pradesh Financial Corporation as both being statutory 
corporations. 
5.1.4.2 Interest Coverage Ratio 
Interest Coverage Ratio is used to determine the ability of a Company to pay interest 
on outstanding debt and is calculated by dividing a company’s Earnings before Interest 
and Taxes (EBIT) by interest expenses of the same period. The lower the ratio,  the 
lesser will be the ability of the company to pay interest on debt. An interest coverage 
ratio of below one indicates that the Company was not generating sufficient revenues 
to meet its expenses on interest. The details of interest coverage ratio in SPSEs which 
had interest burden are given below in Table 5.9.

5.1.5.4 Rate of real return on Government investment (RoRR) 
In order to bring the historical cost of investments to its present value at the end of each 
year up to 31 March 2023, the past investments / year-wise funds infused by the GoHP 
in the SPSEs have been compounded at the year -wise average rate of interest on 
Government borrowings which is considered as the minimum cost of funds to the 
Government, for the concerned year. Therefore, present value of the State Government 
investment was computed where funds had been infused by the State Government in 
the shape of equity, interest free loans and grants/subsidie s for operational and 
administrative expenses (O&AE), if any, less disinvestments since inception of these 
Companies till 31 March 2023. 
The present value of the State Government investment in SPSEs was computed on the 
basis of the following assumptions: 
• Interest free loans have been considered as investment infusion by the State 
Government. Further, in those cases where interest free loans given to the SPSEs 
were later converted into equity, the amount of loan converted into equity has been 
deducted from the amount of interest free loans and added to the equity of that year. 
• The average rate of interest on Government borrowings for the concerned financial 
year17 was adopted as compounded rate for arriving at the Present Value since these 
represent the cost incurred by the Government towards investment of funds for the 
year and therefore is considered as the minimum expected rate of return on 
investments made by the Government. 
• Disinvestment has been deducted while calculating total investment at the end of 
the year. 
 
17 Calculation for the average rate for interest paid = Interest Payment/ [(Amount of previous year’s 
Fiscal Liabilities + Current year's Fiscal Liabilities)/2]*100.

5.1.5.6 Return on Equity for SPSEs 
Return on Equity 19  (RoE) is a measure of financial performance to assess how 
effectively a company’s assets are being used to create profit. RoE is calculated by 
dividing net income (i.e., net profit after taxes) by shareholders’ fund. It is expressed as 
a percentage and can be calculated for any company if net income and shareholders' 
fund are both positive numbers.  
The RoE of the profit earning 13 working SPSEs stood at 14.65 per cent20 in 2022-23.

5.2 Oversight role of C&AG 
 
5.2.1 Audit of State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs) 
Comptroller & Auditor General of India (C&AG) appoints the Statutory Auditors of a 
Government Company (GC) and Government Controlled Other Company (GCOC) 
under Section 139  (5) and (7) of the Companies Act, 2013. C&AG has the right to 
conduct a supplementar y audit and issue comments upon or supplement the Audit 
Report of the Statutory Auditor. Further, the Statutes governing some Statutory 
Corporations (SCs) require that their accounts be audited by the C&AG and a report be 
submitted to the Legislature.  
5.2.2 Appointment of Statutory Auditors of State Public Sector Enterprises by 
C&AG  
Section 139 (5) of the Companies Act, 2013 provides that the Statutory Auditors in case 
of a Government Company or Government Controlled Other Company are to be 
appointed by the C&AG within a period of 180 days from the commencement of the 
financial year.  
The Statutory Auditors of such Companies of Himachal Pradesh for the year 2022 -23 
were appointed by the C&AG between September 2022 and April 2024 as per the 
details given in Appendix 5.10. 

Chapter V: Financial Performance of State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs) 
 
121 | P a g e  
5.2.3 Submission of accounts by SPSEs 
 
5.2.3.1 Need for timely submission 
According to Section 394 (1) (a) & (b) of the Companies Act, 2013, Annual Report on 
the working and affairs of a Government Company is to be prepared within three 
months of its Annual General Meeting (AGM) and as soon as may be after such 
preparation, laid before the legislature together with a copy of the Audit Report and any 
comments upon or supplement to the Audit Report made by the C&AG. Almost similar 
provisions exist in the respective Acts regulating Statutory Corporations. This 
mechanism provides the  necessary legislative control over the utilisation of public 
funds invested in the companies from the Consolidated Fund of State. 
Section 96(1) of the Companies Act, 2013 requires every Company to hold AGM of 
the shareholders once in every calendar year. It is also stated that not more than 
15 months shall elapse between the date of one AGM and that of the next. Further, 
Section 129 (2) of the Companies Act, 2013 stipulates that the audited Financial 
Statement for the financial year has to be placed in the said AGM for consideration.  
Section 129 (7) of the Companies Act, 2013 also provides for levy of penalty like fine 
and imprisonment on the persons including Directors of the Company responsible for 
non-compliance with the provisions of Section 129 of the Companies Act, 2013.  
Despite the above provisions, annual accounts of several SPSEs were pending for audit 
as on 30 September 2023, as detailed in the following paragraphs.  
5.2.3.2 Timeliness in preparation of accounts by Government Companies and 
Government Controlled Other Companies 
As of 31 March 2023, there were 23 Government Companies and four 21 Government 
Controlled Other Companies in the state of Himachal Pradesh under the purview of 
C&AG’s audit. The accounts for the year 2022 -23 were due from all the 27 SPSEs.

5.2.3.3 Timeliness in preparation of accounts by Statutory Corporation 
Audit of Statutory Corporations is governed by their respective Legislatures. 
Accordingly, Audit of two24 Statutory Corporations in Himachal Pradesh is required to 
be conducted by the C&AG. Out of these, for one 25 Statutory Corporation, C&AG is 
the sole Auditor. None of these Statutory Corporations presented the accounts for the 
year 2022-23 for audit before 30 September 2023. Thus, on 30 September 2023, two 
accounts of two Statutory Corporations were pending.

5.2.4 C&AG’s oversight- Audit of Accounts and Supplementary audit  
 
5.2.4.1 Financial reporting framework 
Companies are required to prepare the Financial Statements in the format laid down in 
Schedule III of the Companies Act 2013 26  and in adherence to the mandatory 
Accounting Standards prescribed by the Central Government, in consultation with 
National Advisory Committee on Accounting Standards renamed as National Financial 
Reporting Authority. The Statutory Corporations are required to prepare their accounts 
as per the format prescribed under the rules framed in consultation with the C&AG and 
any other specific provisions relating to accounts provided in the Act governing such 
Corporations. 
5.2.4.2 Audit of accounts of SPSEs by Statutory Auditors  
The Statutory Auditors appointed by the C&AG under Section 139 of the Companies 
Act 2013 conduct audit of accounts of the Government Companies and submit their 
report thereon in accordance with Section 143 of the Companies Act, 2013. 
The C&AG plays an oversight role by monitoring the performance of the Statutory 
Auditors in conducting the audit of State Public Sector Enterprises with the overall 
objective that the Statutory Auditors discharge the functions assigned to them properly 
and effectively. This function is discharged by exercising the power: 
• to issue directions to the Statutory Auditors under Section 143 (5) of the 
Companies Act, 2013; and  
• to supplement or comment upon the Statutory Auditor's report under Section 
143 (6) of the Companies Act, 2013. 
5.2.4.3 Supplementary Audit of accounts of SPSEs  
The prime responsibility for the preparation of financial statements in accordance with 
the financial reporting framework prescribed under the Companies Act, 2013 or other 
relevant Act is of the management of an entity. 
The Statutory Auditors appointed by the C&AG under section 139 of the Companies 
Act, 2013 are responsible for expressing an opinion on the financial statements under 
Section 143 of the Companies Act, 2013 based on independent audit in accordance with 
the Auditing and Assurance Standards (AAS) of Institute of Chartered Accountants of 
India and sub directions given by the C&AG. The Statutory Auditors are required to 
submit the Audit Report to the C&AG under Section 143 of the Companies Act, 2013. 
The certified accounts of the selected Government Companies along with the report of 
the Statutory Auditors are reviewed by C&AG by carrying out a supplementary audit. 
Based on such review, significant audit observations, if any, are reported under 
Section 143 (6) of the Companies Act, 2013 to be placed before the Annual General 
Meeting.  
 
26  As amended up to March 2021.

5.2.5 Result of C&AG’s Oversight Role  
 
5.2.5.1 Audit of Accounts of Government Companies and Government 
Controlled Other Companies under Section 143 of the Companies Act, 
2013 
Twenty four Financial Statements for the year 2021 -22 and previous years (from 
2015-16 to 2020-21) were received from 17 companies between 01  October 2022 and 
30 September 2023. Twenty six Financial Statements27 of 19 companies were reviewed 
in audit by the C&AG and a Non-Review Certificate was issued to one account of one 
SPSE (Himachal Pradesh General Industries Corporation Limited for the year 2021-22) 
as detailed in Appendix 5.12. The results of the review are detailed below:  
(i) Result of Supplementary Audit  
As a result of the Supplementary Audit conducted in 19 SPSEs, a number of 
quantitative as well as qualitative changes were made by the SPSEs in their financial 
statements which led to an improvement in the quality of their financial statements. The 
net fin ancial impact as a result of the Supplementary Audit was to the tune of 
₹150.76 crore28 on profitability and ₹294.74 crore29 on assets/liabilities as depicted in 
Chart 5.2.

During 2022 -23, there was no case of Government Companies or Government 
Controlled Other Companies amending their financial statements as a result of the 
comments of C&AG.

During 2022-23, there was no case of revision of statutory auditors’ report as a result

The depreciation in respect of carried forward opening balance of

3 HP Agro Industries 
Corporation Limited 
(2021-22) 
i) The short term provision head includes a provision of ₹97.16 lakh 
(March 2013) in respect of interest and damages levied by EPFO 
for delayed deposit of CPF of employees for the period from March 
2007 to July 2012. The EPFO revised (May 2015) damages and 
interest amount to ₹77.06 lakh. However, the provision in this 
regard has not been revised. This has resulted in overstatement of 
above head and understatement of profit by ₹20.10 lakh. 
ii)Deferred tax assets were shown as ₹16.15 lakh., however, taking 
into consideration the timing difference in respect of depreciation 
on assets, provision for gratuity and provision for leave encashment 
which are capable of reversal in one or subsequent p eriods, the 
deferred tax assets as on 31st March 2022 works out to be ₹30.41 
lakh. Thus, incorrect recognition of deferred tax amount resulted in 
understatement of deferred tax assets and understatement of profit 
by ₹14.26 lakh. 
4 HP Tourism 
Development 
Corporation Limited 
(2017-18) 
i) The other expenses do not include ₹10.50 lakh being the service 
tax paid (March 2015) against the total demand of ₹21 lakh (₹10.50 
lakh principal +₹10.50 lakh as penalty) which was booked as 
advances under the Assets head. The demand was settled (Januar y 
2020) with service tax of ₹10.50 lakh under Sabka Viswas (Legacy 
Dispute Resolution) Scheme, 2019. As the service tax liability was 
finalised before the approval of these financial statements (March 
2021), the same should have been charged to expenditure  as per 
Accounting Standard 4, which has not been done. This has resulted 
in an understatement of other expenses and overstatement of 
Advances by ₹10.50 lakh. Loss for the year was also been 
understated by ₹10.50 lakh.

Government of India' head and credited the 'Fixed Assets – 
Tangible Assets' head. This resulted in understatement of 
'Depreciation and Amorti sation Expenses' and 'Other Income' by 
₹2.03 crore.

through bank directly against notice issued for the A.Y 2012 -13.

5.2.7 Management Letters  
One of the objectives of the financial audit is to establish communication on matters 
arising from the audit of financial statements between the auditors and those charged 
with the responsibility of the governance of the corporate entity.  
The material observations on the Financial Statements of SPSEs/SCs were reported as 
comments by the C&AG under Section 143 (6)(b) of the Companies Act, 2013. Besides 
these comments, irregularities or deficiencies observed by the C&AG in the financial 
reports or in the reporting process were also communicated to the management through 

Composite Audit Report for the period ended March 2023 
134 | P a g e  
‘Management Letters’ for taking corrective action. These deficiencies generally related 
to: 
• Adjustments arising out of audit that could have a significant effect on the 
financial statements; and  
• Inadequate or non-disclosure of certain information on which the management 
of the concerned SPSEs gave assurances that corrective action would be taken 
in the subsequent year. 
During the year, Management Letters were issued to 15 SPSEs as per details given in 
Appendix 5.14 and one Statutory Corporation (Himachal Road Transport 
Corporation). 
5.3 Corporate Governance 
 
5.3.1 Introduction 
Corporate Governance is a system of structuring, operating and controlling an 
organisation with a view to achieving long term strategic goals to satisfy the stakeholder 
(shareholders, employees, customers, suppliers, government and community) and 
complying with the legal and regulatory requirements. Corporate Governance is a way 
of directing and controlling companies. It is concerned with the morals, ethics, values, 
parameters, conduct and behaviour of the company and management. It is the system 
by which companies are directed and controlled by the management in the best interests 
of the shareholders and other stakeholders ensuring greater transparency and better 
timely financial reporting. The absence of good governance structures and lack of 
adherence to the governance principles increases the risk of corruption and misuse of 
entrusted power by the management in public sector. 
5.3.1.1 Provisions as contained in the Companies Act, 2013 
The Companies Act, 2013 was enacted on 29 August 2013, replacing the Companies 
Act, 1956.  In addition, the Ministry of Corporate Affairs has also notified 
(31 March 2014) Companies Rules, 2014 on Management and Administration, 
Appointment and Qualification of Directors, Meetings of Board and its powers and 
Accounts. The Companies Act, 2013 toge ther with the Companies Rules provide a 
robust framework for Corporate Governance. The requirements inter alia provide for: 
• Qualifications for Independent Directors along with the duties and guidelines for 
professional conduct {Section 149(6) read with Rule 5 of the Companies 
(Appointment and Qualification of Directors) Rules, 2014}.  
• Mandatory appointment of at least one Woman Director on the Board. Section 
149 (1) of the Companies Act, 2013, read with Rule 3 of the Companies 
(Appointment and Qualification of Directors) Rules 2014, provides that every 
listed Company and every other Public Company having paid–up share capital of 
100 crore or more; or turnover of ₹300 crore or more shall appoint at least one  
woman director in its Board. 

Chapter V: Financial Performance of State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs) 
 
135 | P a g e  
• Mandatory establishment of Audit Committee {Section 177(1)}, Nomination and 
Remuneration Committee {178(1)} and Stakeholders Relation Committee 
{Section 178(5)}. 
• Holding of a minimum of four meetings of Board of Directors every year in such 
a manner that not more than 120 days shall intervene between two consecutive 
meetings of the Board {Section 173(1)}.  
5.3.1.2 SEBI guidelines on Corporate Governance 
With the enactment of Companies Act, 2013, Securities and Exchange Board of India 
(SEBI) amended (April and September 2014), Clause 49 of the Listing Agreement to 
align it with the Corporate Governance provisions specified in the Companies Act, 
2013. SEBI further issued (13 October 2015) a uniform listing agreement format for all 
types of securities which required the listed entity to comply with the provisions of 
SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015. These 
regulations were amended30 from time to time. 
5.3.1.3 Review of Compliance of the Corporate Governance provisions by 
selected State Public Sector Enterprises 
As on 31 March 2023, there were 30 State Public Sector Enterprises (SPSEs) under the 
audit jurisdiction of the C&AG of India, out of which two SPSEs 31 are statutory 
corporations, three SPSEs 32  are inactive (including one SPSE 33  which is under 
liquidation since 2000-01). Out of balance 25 SPSEs, 12 SPSEs are registered as public 
and balance 13 are listed as private class of companies. List of all SPSEs including their 
administrative departments and type of company is given in Appendix 5.15. The 
review covers 25 unlisted SPSEs including one debt listed SPSE 34 for the year ended 
31 March 2023. SPSEs established under an Act other than the Companies Act have 
been excluded from the review. Further, inactive companies which operate only with 
skeleton management structure have also been excluded.  
5.3.2 Composition of Board of Directors 
The board is a governing body of either elected or appointed persons that meets at 
regular intervals to set policies for corporate management and oversees the activities of 
an organisation. Section  2 (10) of the Companies Act, 2013 defined that “Board of 
Directors” or “Board”, in relation to a company, means the collective body of the 
directors of the company. 
5.3.2.1 Independent Directors 
The presence of independent representatives on the Board, capable of taking an 
independent view on the decisions of the management is widely considered as a means 
 
30  25th May 2016, 8th July 2016, 4th January 2017 and 15th February 2017.
